Web research is available through the aii-web-tools skill, in three levels (broad → specific):

1. web search — Returns titles, URLs, snippets. Use first to discover and scan the landscape.
2. web fetch — Reads a page and returns its content as markdown (HTML or PDF). Use to understand a source. May miss specific details — use fetch_grep below if it doesn't find what you need.
3. fetch_grep — Regex search over a page/PDF's full text. Returns exact matching sections with context. Use for precise details, exact numbers, methodology, or PDFs.

- Search then fetch results → sequential (need URLs first)

3. TRIANGULATE - Cross-reference claims across multiple independent sources
4. ACKNOWLEDGE UNCERTAINTY - Be honest about confidence levels and limitations
5. SYNTHESIZE - Produce a coherent answer that accounts for conflicting evidence

  \n## QUALITY CHECKS\n\nAfter each phase:\n1. Verify extractions with multiple sources\n2. Cross-check recommendations against\
  \ recent published studies\n3. Ensure coverage assessment matches stated sample (post-1990 democratizers)\n4. Validate that\
  \ GMM specification guidance aligns with methodological literature"
explanation: |-
  This research is critical for the dual stratification hypothesis investigation because:

  1. **Measurement Validity Threat**: The hypothesis relies on education inequality as a key variable, but direct measures (education Gini) may not be available in OWID panels. Validating proxy measures (tertiary enrollment, mean years) against established education inequality metrics is essential to ensure the empirical test measures what it claims to measure.

  2. **Specification Sensitivity**: Arellano-Bond GMM models with interaction terms are methodologically challenging. Incorrect specification could lead to biased estimates or invalid inference. The research will provide exact guidance on how to correctly specify the dual stratification interaction in dynamic panel models.

  3. **Data Source Selection**: The choice between SWIID and World Bank PIP for income inequality, and the validation of V-Dem's political equality index, directly affects the credibility and replicability of the findings. This research will provide evidence-based recommendations.

  4. **Acemoglu Calibration**: As the reviewer is Daron Acemoglu, the research must meet high standards of methodological rigor and institutional economics grounding. The GMM specification and measurement validation must align with established practices in the literature Acemoglu engages with.

  5. **Reproducibility on OWID**: The requirement to use Our World in Data panels means the research must verify that recommended measures are actually available in OWID and document how to access them.

  Without this research, the subsequent experiment iteration risks using invalid proxies, misspecified models, or inappropriate data sources, which would undermine the hypothesis test and potentially lead to incorrect conclusions about the dual stratification effect.
</artifact_plan>

<investigation_process>
1. DIVERGE: Brainstorm multiple angles/framings of the question before searching. Think across fields — what adjacent domains might have relevant insights?
2. SEARCH: Multiple queries per angle with different phrasings to discover the landscape
3. FETCH: Read promising URLs at high level. Snippets are NOT enough — fetch full pages
4. DETAIL: aii-web-tools fetch_grep for specifics from key pages/PDFs
5. CONTRAST: Actively try to disprove your emerging conclusions. Search with different phrasings, "[topic] criticism", "[topic] limitations". Check across fields — the same finding may exist under different names
6. SYNTHESIZE: Integrate into balanced conclusion
7. ITERATE: Expect to repeat steps 2-6 if findings are incomplete or one-sided. Don't settle on first results
8. SUMMARIZE: Output JSON must include 'title' and 'summary' fields
</investigation_process>

<output_requirements>
- Write research_out.json to your workspace with all findings
- Provide your finding as clear prose WITH NUMBERED CITATIONS
- EVERY factual claim must have a citation number in brackets: [1], [2], [1, 3], etc.
- Include BOTH supporting AND contradicting evidence
- Be explicit about confidence level and what would change it
- End with follow-up questions for further investigation
</output_requirements>

Something genuinely novel and groundbreaking that traces how inequality, education, and democratic-quality co-evolve across post-1990 democratizers, identifies what sustains resilience versus backsliding, and tests whether welfare-state institutions mediate the link. MUST use Our World in Data (OWID) panels.

Ambition: level 1 of 5 — confirmatory/parametric science: a careful confirmatory test within established institutional-economics theory; precision, identification, and robustness over conceptual novelty.

Reviewer: I am Daron Acemoglu (MIT). Calibrate from my existing work. Cross-domain perspectives (historical sociology, political behavior, economic anthropology) welcome — but keep them close to what I already know, not too far from my background.

Submission/Goal: a paper for the American Political Science Review, the Journal of Democracy, or World Politics. Audience: comparative political economists and political scientists. Tone: empirically rigorous, grounded in institutional economics, reproducible on public OWID data.

index to measure inequality in educational attainment. They present two methods (direct and indirect) for calculating an education Gini index, and generate a quinquennial data set on education Gini indexes for the over-15-population in 85 countries (1960-90). Preliminary empirical analysis suggests that: 1) Inequality in education in most of the countries declined over the three decades, with a few exceptions. 2) Inequality in education as measured by the education Gini index is negatively associated with average years of schooling, implying that countries with higher educational attainment are more likely to achieve equality in education, than those with lower attainment. 3) A clear pattern of an education Kuznets curve exists if the standard deviation of education is used. 4) Gender gaps are clearly related to education inequality, and over time, the association between gender gaps, and inequality becomes stronger. 5) Increases in per capita GDP (adjusted for purchasing power parity) seem to be negatively associated with education inequality, and positively related to labor force's average years of schooling, after controlling for initial income levels.\n\n##### Link to Data Set\n\n##### Citation\n\n\u201cThomas, Vinod; Wang, Yan; Fan, Xibo. 2001. Measuring Education Inequality : Gini Coefficients of Education. Policy Research Working Paper;No. 2525. \u00a9 http://hdl.handle.net/10986/19738 License: [CC BY 3.0 IGO](http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/3.0/igo/).\u201d\n\n##### Digital Object Identifier\n\n[ https://doi.org/10.1596/1813-9450-2525](https://doi.org/10.1596/1813-9450-2525)\n\n##### URI\n\n[ https://hdl.handle.net/10986/19738](https://hdl.handle.net/10986/19738)\n\n##### Associated URLs\n\n##### Associated content\n\n##### Report Series\n\nReport Series\n\n[Policy Research Working Papers](/entities/series/26e071dc-b0bf-409c-b982-df2970295c87)\n\n __Show more\n\n##### Other publications in this report series\n\n  * Publication\n\n[One-Fifth of the World\u2019s Population Is at High Risk of Climate-Related Hazards](/entities/publication/379cd5a7-54cb-425a-8043-38f596fecac7)\n\n(Washington, DC: World Bank, 2026-05-15) Hill, Ruth; Gorgulu, Nisan; Brunckhorst, Ben; Nguyen, Minh Cong; Hallegatte, Stephane; Naikal, Esther\n\n __Show more\n\nClimate change is increasing the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events. When these events occur, they threaten lives and livelihoods. This paper estimates the global population at high risk of climate-related hazards by combining household-level vulnerability data with local exposure to four types of events: agricultural droughts in rural areas, floods, heatwaves, and cyclones. Under current climate conditions, 4.5 billion people are expected to experience these hazards at intensities exceeding hazard-specific thresholds chosen to capture the likely occurrence of systemic impacts within a lifetime. One-third of this population is considered highly vulnerable, based on seven dimensions that influence ability to cope and recover: income, education, access to finance, social protection, drinking water, electricity, and access to services and markets. Overall, one in five people globally are considered at high risk, meaning they are both likely to experience at least one of these hazards and face severely limited capacity to recover from their impacts. Although the share of the global population at high risk has nearly halved since 2010 due to decreased vulnerability, the number of people exposed has increased, and progress has been uneven across regions. This study introduces a new global population headcount indicator based on household survey data and high-resolution spatial data to monitor climate risks across countries and over time.\n\n__Show more\n\n  * Publication\n\n[Worldwide Job Losses Due to Natural Hazards](/entities/publication/084a67f2-6925-41dc-aad4-ac8d0a11ec57)\n\n(Washington, DC: World Bank, 2026-05-29) Isah, Abdulrasheed; Rentschler, Jun; Middelanis, Robin; Avner, Paolo; Hallegatte, Stephane\n\n __Show more\n\nNatural hazards can profoundly disrupt economies, yet their impact on employment remains underexplored. This study quantifies job losses due to floods, earthquakes, wind, storm surges, tsunamis, and heat across 132 countries, using a full-time job equivalent loss estimation approach. The results show that fast-onset natural shocks cause 9.4 million job equivalent losses annually on average, predominantly due to earthquakes and floods, with burdens concentrated in East Asia and the Pacific and Sub-Saharan Africa. Additionally, extreme heat was associated with 79.7 million job equivalent losses annually across 114 countries between 2015 and 2024, with the burdens concentrated in South Asia and Sub-Saharan Africa. Yet, average annual job losses can be significantly lower than losses from specific extreme events, for instance, with 1-in-100-year hazard events resulting in losses that exceed average annual job losses by a factor of over 10. Overall, low-income countries experience the highest job loss rate per capita. Within countries, the poorest population group bears a disproportionate share of job equivalent losses. Results highlight the urgent need for targeted adaptation and resilience measures that safeguard workers, jobs, and productivity to support economic development.\n\n__Show more\n\n  * Publication\n\n[The Cycle of Hate, and What We Can Do About It](/entities/publication/d64827a1-26e5-4836-9809-9d88fc0aab50)\n\n(Washington, DC: World Bank, 2026-02-09) Esses, Victoria; Harell, Allison; Lowe, Matt; Markus, Hazel Rose; Mills, Aaron; Rao, Manasi; Rao, Vijayendra; Reicher, Stephen; Singh, Prerna\n\n __Show more\n\nIntergroup hate\u2014both shaped by and shaping development processes\u2014is spreading worldwide as hate speech becomes normalized, hate groups proliferate, and political discourse increasingly frames opponents as enemies rather than as partners in compromise. Drawing on historical, economic, political, and social-psychological research, this paper synthesizes 10 drivers of intergroup hate into four interlocking components: history, current context, call to arms, and justification of mistreatment. These components form a self-reinforcing cycle that escalates animosity and legitimizes harm, making hate difficult\u2014but not impossible\u2014to disrupt. The paper shows how the 10 drivers interact over time and uses the cycle of hate framework to organize evidence from experiments and program evaluations aimed at reducing intergroup animosity. This evidence indicates that intergroup hate can be interrupted at multiple points through coordinated psychosocial, institutional, and economic interventions. By contrast, policies that neglect any of the four components\u2014particularly elite and media mobilization\u2014consistently underperform. Context sensitive, integrated, institutionally embedded strategies hold the greatest promise, including the potential to support inoculation and early-warning systems that detect and counter intergroup hate before it is politically mobilized.\n\n__Show more\n\n  * Publication\n\n[The World Is Developing at Its Slowest Pace in 75 Years](/entities/publication/c97258fc-bf8f-4bd7-b56f-347b8349368d)\n\n(Washington, DC: World Bank, 2026-04-13) Mahler, Daniel Gerszon; Serajuddin, Umar; Wadhwa, Divyanshi; Yonzan, Nishant\n\n __Show more\n\nUnderstanding and accurately measuring societal progress is crucial for guiding policy and development efforts. This paper introduces a new method to measure societal progress that is comparable across different stages of development, time periods, and indicators. The paper utilizes this method to analyze six key indicators of development with cross-country data from 1950 and finds that the world is developing at its slowest pace in 75 years. This slowdown has already caused large development losses and will cause global poverty to increase if it continues. The paper identifies 54 countries that are more than 100 years away from reaching standards associated with graduating to developed country status if they continue at their current pace. The framework can be used to set development targets at various levels of ambition for every country and globally. The method involves specific data requirements, which means it is not suitable in all contexts.\n\n__Show more\n\n  * Publication\n\n[Assessing The Real-World Economic Value of Weather Forecasts under Compounding Extremes: A Decision-Specific Framework](/entities/publication/066ecb23-4735-4440-80ad-5149048dcc49)\n\n(Washington, DC: World Bank, 2026-06-02) Olivetti, Leonardo; Messori, Gabriele; Avner, Paolo; Hallegatte, Stephane\n\n __Show more\n\nAssessing the real-world economic value of weather forecasts remains challenging, particularly in the context of high-impact extreme events. Although meteorological skill has improved substantially in recent years\u2014driven by steady advances in physics-based models and impressive breakthroughs in artificial intelligence-based forecasting\u2014operational evaluations still focus primarily on standard skill metrics, with limited consideration of how improvements in meteorological skill translate into economic value. This study proposes a flexible framework to assess the economic value of weather forecasts, with penalty functions that explicitly account for compounding losses as well as declining user trust in cases of repeated false alarms. In addition, the framework allows for varying cost\u2014loss ratios to represent heterogeneous prevention costs and vulnerability structures. The framework is applied to cities exposed to weather-related natural hazards, comparing the relative economic value of leading physics-based and data-driven forecasting systems from the European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts. The value of forecasts is highly sensitive to assumptions about compounding losses, penalty structures, and prevention costs, which often substantially alter conclusions drawn from meteorological skill alone. For instance, in some cities in Southern Europe, the higher sensitivity of the physics-based Integrated Forecast System high-resolution model (IFS HRES) makes it better suited when protection costs are small relative to potential losses, while the higher specificity of the data-driven Artificial Intelligence Forecasting System (AIFS) makes it better when protection costs are higher. These findings underscore the importance of evaluating economic value under realistic risk scenarios to ensure that improvements in predictive accuracy translate into meaningful societal and economic benefits.\n\n__Show more\n\n\n\n\n[Show more](javascript:void\\(0\\);)\n\n##### Journal\n\n##### Journal Volume\n\n##### Journal Issue\n\n##### Collections\n\n[Policy Research Working Papers](/collections/76a0df2a-d133-5d52-ba12-6cb522e6475c)\n\n[ __Full item page](/entities/publication/50558cd4-59e3-5742-b78e-7a4afac5fbf7/full)\n\nShow statistical information\n\n### Related items\n\nShowing items related by metadata.\n\n  * Publication\n\n[Republic of Colombia : Improving Opportunities for Education II](/entities/publication/98331d55-f676-5e4c-b935-2a17796dd1bf)\n\n(Washington, DC, 2013-11-27) World Bank\n\n __Show more\n\nThis note presents the achievements in second phase of the Programmatic Knowledge Services (PKS) for improving opportunities for education in Colombia and is the final report of the PKS series. The PKS was fully aligned with the country's National Development Plan and with the Bank country partnership strategy. The PKS was a multi-year program, covering FY12-FY14 and was implemented in two phases. This note presents the actions undertaken under second phase (carried out in FY13) and closes the PKS series. The FY14 strategy for the Bank's education program will focus on new lending. Colombia's education sector does not adequately prepare people for a competitive, global labor market. This is an\nextension of our paper, \"Measuring Educational Inequality: Education Gini Index from 1960 to 1990.\"\nmimeo, World Bank Institute. September 27, 1999. Washington D.C. L6pez\nfor collaborating a paper on this topic using data from 20 countries, Nancy Birdsall, Shaohua Chen, Deon\nFilmer, Emmanuel Jimenz, Peter Moock, Martin Ravallion and The Quality of Growth team, for their\ncomments and suggestions, and the Bank's Research Committee for providing a part of the funding. The\nfindings, interpretations and conclusions expressed here are those of the authors. They do not necessarily\nrepresent the views of the World Bank, its Executive Directors or the countries they represent. Comments\nand questions should be sent to the corresponding authors, Yan Wang at vwang2( worldbank.or2 and/or\nXibo Fan at gini@egroups.com. The dataset is available at the World Bank's web page\nhttp://www.worldbank.org/devforum/forum\nqog3.html.\n\n\n1. Introduction\nEqual access to education is among the basic human rights to which everyone is entitled. Yet,\nthe educational gaps between various groups in many countries are staggering, as shown by\nmany studies. If people's abilities are normally distributed, then a skewed distribution of\neducation opportunities represents large welfare losses. As with land and machinery, an\nequitable distribution of human capital (basic literacy and nutrition/health) constitutes a\nprecondition for individual productivity and ability to rise above poverty.' Furthermore, an\nequitable distribution of opportunities is preferable to a redistribution of existing assets or\nincomes. This is because education builds new assets and improves social welfare by its spill-\nover effect, without making anyone worse off. Ensuring access to educational opportunities by\nattending to both the supply and demand sides, is a win-win policy gaining support in industrial\nand developing countries. To support such an effort, an indicator that can be easily calculated and\nmonitored over time would be useful.\nThis paper aims at developing a measure for educational inequality, using the concept of\neducation Gini index based on school attainment data, for a large number of countries over time.\nEducation Gini could be used as one of the indicators of welfare, complementing average\neducational attainment, health and nutrition, income per capita, and other indicators of welfare.\nAfter reviewing the literature and discussing the methodology, we look into the relationship\nbetween the education Gini index and average educational attainment, gender gaps, and the\nstandard deviation of education. As a narrowly focused technical paper, we do not attempt to\nfind a causal relationship between inequality in education and growth, as they could be jointly\ndetermined and mutually reinforcing.\nStandard deviations and Gini coefficients are often chosen as measures of inequality.\nStandard divisions of school attainment were used in a few studies. Only four previous studies\nwere found to have used Gini coefficients in measuring education inequality. They estimated the\nGini coefficient based on either enrollment or education finance. To properly measure education\ninequality, a Gini index should be based on educational attainment data. However, to our\nknowledge, no study has estimated education Gini coefficient based on educational attainment\nfor a large number of industrial and developing countries. (See table 1 for a selective literature\nreview.)\n2. Indicators to Measure Various Aspects of Education\nVarious indicators have been used to measure different aspects of education in cross country\nanalyses. These indicators include, among others, enrollment ratios, educational attainment,\nquality by input of resources, and quality by cognitive test scores. On the distribution of\n' There was a heated debate over the \"Equity of what?\" Amartya Sen (1980) sees individual's levels of functionings,\nsuch as literacy and nutrition, as attributes to be equalized. Others see the opportunities people face as the attributes\nto be equalized (Arneson 1989, Cohen 1989, and Roemer 1993). Yet others consider the amount of resources as the\nattribute to be equalized (Dworkin 1981).\n3\n\neducation, standard deviation of years of schooling was used, and lately, the education Gini\nindex, as a new indicator to measure education inequality. This section briefly explains the\nusefulness and problems of the various indicators that measure different aspects of education.\nFlow variable: Enrollment Ratios. At the early stage, the enrollment ratios for different\nlevels of schooling were used as indicators of human development (Barro 1991, Mankiw et al\n1992, Levine and Renelt 1992, Levine and Zervos 1993). The most commonly used are the\nprimary- and secondary enrollment ratios. One problem of this approach is that enrollment ratio\nonly measures the flow of population's education or access to education. It does not show the\ncumulated educational attainment/outcome. As a result, it is often inappropriate to use these\nenrollment ratios in growth models. Measuring education inequality based on enrollment data is\nalso problematic as they do not reflect the stock of human capital.\nStock variable: Attainment measured by Number of Average Years of Schooling.\nPsacharopoulos and Arriagada (1986) suggested that the proper indicator of human development\nlevel is the stock of educational attainment defined as average years of schooling.\nPsacharopoulos and Arriagada (1986) made the efforts of collecting the census information on\neach country's schooling distribution over the entire population, and calculated educational\nattainment. Barro and Lee (1991, 1993, and 1997) gathered more data and formalized the use of\neducational attainment for growth regressions. Nehru, Swanson, and Dubey (1994) also created a\ncross country database for educational attainment, through estimating the schooling distributions\nover time for various countries. Measuring the distribution of education based on these\nattainment data becomes feasible.\nThe Quality of Schooling.\nEducational attainment across countries may not be\ncomparable as the quality of schooling differ widely. Behrman and Birdsall (1983) and others\n(Lockheed and Verspoor 1991; Card and Krueger 1992) warned that quantity alone is not\nenough, quality must be taken into consideration when measuring the level of human\ndevelopment. Two typical approaches were used to measure the quality of education, the input\napproach and the output approach, each with its own problems and limitations.\nThe Input Approach (Resources for Schooling). One way to measure the education\nquality is to see which country devotes more resources to education than others. Resources being\ninputted into the education systems can be measured by pupil-teacher ratio, by expenditures on\nteachers' wage, by spending on book and reading materials. One problem is that high volume of\ninput does not necessarily yield high quality. Another problem is that the inputs for schools are\nnot independent of the income. There is limited feasibility of using inputs of schooling as proxies\nfor education quality (Hanushek and Kim 1995).\nThe Output Approach (Test Score of Cognitive Performance). The output approach\ndirectly measures the achievements of schooling by comparing the scores of cognitive\nperformance, which the students of the same-age group of various countries obtained through the\nsame international tests on the same subjects including mathematics and science. The tests to\nassess student achievement in mathematics and science were conducted both by the International\nAssociation for Evaluation of Educational Achievement (IEA) and by international Assessment\n4\n\nof Fiducation progress (1EAP).2 Two problems that prevented these measures to be widely used\ninclude that first, they are only available for a dozen, mostly industrial, countries, and second,\nthey are not comparable over time. It is for these reasons, we cannot use them to control for the\nquality of education in the education Gini index that we constructed.\nThe Distribution of Education: why important. The distributional dimension of education\nis extremely important for both welfare consideration and for production. If an asset, say physical\ncapital, is freely traded across firms in a competitive environment, its marginal product will be\nequalized through free-market mechanism. As a result, its contribution to output will not be\naffected by its distribution across firms or individuals. If an asset is not completely tradable,\nhowever, then the marginal product of the asset across individuals is not equalized, and there is\nan aggregation problem. In this case, aggregate production finction depends not only on the\naverage level of the asset but also on its distribution. Because education/skill is only partially\ntradable, the average level of educational attainment alone is not sufficient to reflect the\ncharacteristics of a country's human capital. We need to look beyond averages and investigate\nboth the absolute dispersion and the relative dispersion of human capital.\nStandard Deviation of Schooling: Absolute Dispersion. There is a small but growing\nliterature on schooling inequality or the distribution of education (see, for example, Lam and\nLevinson 1991; Londofio 1990; Ram 1990). As data became available for measuring the\ndistribution of education, the disparities became more apparent. Standard deviations have been\nused often to measure the absolute dispersion distribution of assets. Birdsall and Londono\n(1997), investigating the impact of initial asset distribution on growth and poverty reduction,\nfound a significant negative correlation between education dispersion (measured by the standard\ndeviation of schooling) and income growth. In the Inter-American Development Bank (1999)\nstudy on inequality in Latin America, the standard deviation of schooling is used to measure\ninequality of education, and it was found that the larger the standard deviation of schooling, the\ngreater the income inequality--measured by income Gini. (See Table 1 for literature review). Rati\nRam used the standard deviations of schooling to illustrate the existence of an education Kuznuts\ncurve, and concluded that, \"As the average level of schooling rises, educational inequality first\nincreases, and after reaching a peak, starts to decline. The tuming point is about seven years of\neducation\" (Ram 1990). Londofno 1990 also used the same method.\n2 Hanushek and Kim (1995) first transformed the test scores into unified scales, and then utilized the index to test\nhow much of the growth was affected by both the quantity and the quality of education. They found that both the\nquantity and the quality of schooling positively contribute to the growth of income, at statistically significant levels.\nThe output approach measures the quality of education based on student cognitive performance, which was\nregarded by Hanushek and Kim (1995) as a complement to the quantity of education developed by Barro and Lee\n(1993). Lee and Barro (1997) investigated the determinants of school quality, and found that greater school inputs,\nlonger school terms, family background, and strong communities are positively related to student performance.\nHowever, they cannot fully explain the better education outcomes in East Asian countries than in other developing\ncountries. It suggests other factors at play, including those associated with a more open and export-oriented\neconomic environment.\n5\n\nEducation Gini: Measurement of Relative Inequality. Standard deviation of schooling\nonly measures the dispersion of schooling distribution in absolute terms. To measure the relative\ninequality of schooling distribution, developing an indicator for education Gini is necessary.3\nFour previous studies were found to have used Gini coefficient in measuring the\ninequality of education. Education Gini, which are similar to the Gini coefficients widely used to\nmeasure distributions of income, wealth, and land, ranges from 0, which represents perfect\nequality, to 1, which represents perfect inequality. Education Gini coefficients can be calculated\nusing enrollment, financing, or attainment data. Maas and Criel (1982) estimated Gini\ncoefficients based on enrollment data for 16 East African countries. First, they found that the\ndegree of inequality in education opportunity varied enormously from one country to another.\nSecond, enrollment Gini coefficients were negatively related with the average enrollment rate in\na country. In other words, the higher the average enrollment, the lower the inequality. This is\nconsistent with what we found on average educational attainment and education inequality using\na different method. Ter Weele (1975) estimated Gini coefficients using education finance data\nfor several East African countries. Rosthal (1978) summarized four indicators for the distribution\nof education estimated for the United States and Gini index was one of them. Sheret (1982 and\n1988) estimated the Gini coefficient of enrollment for Papua New Guinea. However, the above-\nmentioned Ginis were calculated based on enrollment or education financing, not on the\ndistribution of school attainment.\nIn this paper, we calculate an education Gini index that is based on educational\nattainment of the concerned population (or labor force). Thanks to the painstaking efforts made\nby a group of pioneers including Barro and Lee (1991, 1993, and 1997), Psacharopoulos and\nArriagada (1986), and Nehru, Swanson and Dubey (1995), the data is now available on\nproportions of population with various level of educational attainment for major developing and\nindustrial countries. Lopez, Thomas and Wang (1998) were among the first to try Gini\ncoefficients of education for 12 countries, by utilizing the educational attainment data. The\ndataset was then updated/revised and expanded to 20 countries in a later version (May 1999). In\nthis paper, education Gini indexes are calculated for 85 countries for the period from 1960-1990,\nusing a consistent method which is discussed below.\n3 Both the income Gini and the wealth Gini coefficients have been widely used in studies of growth, poverty and\ninequality. For example, Deininger and Squire (1996) constructed a new data set on inequality in the distribution of\nincome. They found a systematic link between growth and changes in aggregate inequality, and a strong positive\nrelationship between growth and reduction of poverty. Li, Squire, and Zou (1998) tried to explain income inequality\nmainly by three variables: financial depth, civil liberty, and land ownership inequality (land Gini). They found that\nhigher concentration of land contributed to higher income inequality. On the other hand, after adding a dummy\nvariable for the region of Latin America, the Inter-American Development Bank (1999) de-emphasized the\nsignificance of land concentration with respect to income inequality. Lundberg, and Squire (1999) utilized twenty\nvariables of 120 countries, tested various specifications both for poverty and for growth, and come out with two\nstrong conclusions. First, growth is much more sensitive to policy intervention than to inequality is. Second, even a\nmoderate change in inequality coupled certain growth is of tremendous impact on alleviating poverty.\n6\n\n3. Education Gini: Concept and Methodology\nStarting with income Gini coefficient, there are two ways to calculate an income Gini, the direct\nmethod (Deaton 1997) and the indirect method. Mathematically, the direct method states that the\nincome Gini is defined as \"the ratio to the mean of half of the average over all pairs of the\nabsolute deviations between [all possible pairs ofl people\" (Deaton 1997). The indirect method\nfirst constructs the income Lorenz curve, with the cumulative percentage of the income on the\nvertical axis, and the cumulative percentage of the population on the horizontal axis. The forty-\nfive degree line is called the egalitarian line for it represents a completely equal society with\nrespect to the distribution of income. And then the Gini coefficient is calculated as the ratio of\ntwo areas, with the area of the egalitarian triangle as the denominator and the area between\nLorenz curve and"}], "is_error": false, "command": "$PY \"$SKILL_DIR/scripts/aii_fast_web_fetch.py\" fetch --url \"https://openknowledge.worldbank.org/bitstreams/4b17cdf2-54fb-59dd-860c-5966d08a9d72/download\" --max-chars 20000", "exit_code": 0, "timeout": false, "metadata": {"exit_code": 0, "pid": -1, "username": "adrian", "hostname": "vm", "working_dir": "/home/adrian/projects/ai-inventor/aii_data/users/admin/runs/run_-w6fuC_zXl2B/3_invention_loop/iter_1/gen_art/gen_art_research_1", "py_interpreter_path": "/home/adrian/projects/ai-inventor/.venv/bin/python", "prefix": "", "suffix": "\n[The command completed with exit code 0.]"}, "full_output_save_dir": "/home/adrian/projects/ai-inventor/aii_data/users/admin/runs/run_-w6fuC_zXl2B/.oh_sessions/8068fb54ebc9406087c7d39e5d398164/observations", "kind": "TerminalObservation"}

Standardized World Income Inequality Database\n\n\n\n  * [ Income Inequality & Its Consequences, Comparative Political Behavior, and Dynamic Comparative Public Opinion](../res/res/)\n\n\n\n\n# Standardized World Income Inequality Database\n\nCross-national research on the causes and consequences of income inequality has been hindered by the limitations of the existing inequality datasets: greater coverage across countries and over time has been available from these sources only at the cost of significantly reduced comparability across observations. The goal of the Standardized World Income Inequality Database (SWIID) is to meet the needs of those engaged in broadly cross-national research by maximizing the comparability of income inequality data while maintaining the widest possible coverage across countries and over time. It incorporates data from the [OECD Income Distribution Database](https://www.oecd.org/social/income-distribution-database.htm), the [Socio-Economic Database for Latin America and the Caribbean](http://www.cedlas.econo.unlp.edu.ar/wp/en/estadisticas/sedlac/) generated by CEDLAS and the World Bank, [Eurostat](https://ec.europa.eu/eurostat/), the World Bank\u2019s [PovcalNet](http://iresearch.worldbank.org/PovcalNet/home.aspx), the [UN Economic Commission for Latin America and the Caribbean](http://interwp.cepal.org/sisgen/ConsultaIntegrada.asp?idIndicador=250&idioma=e), national statistical offices around the world, and academic studies while minimizing reliance on problematic assumptions by using as much information as possible from proximate years within the same country. The data collected by the [Luxembourg Income Study](http://www.lisdatacenter.org/our-data/lis-database/) is employed as the standard. The SWIID currently incorporates comparable Gini indices of disposable and market income inequality for 199 countries for as many years as possible from 1960 to the present; it also includes information on absolute and relative redistribution. A full description of the SWIID, the procedure used to generate it, and an assessment of the SWIID\u2019s performance in comparison to the available alternatives is presented here:\n\n[Solt, Frederick. 2020. \u201cMeasuring Income Inequality Across Countries and Over Time: The Standardized World Income Inequality Database.\u201d _Social Science Quarterly_ 101(3):1183-1199. SWIID Version 9.92, April 2026.](../res/published/solt2020)\n\nThere are two ways of using the SWIID. To directly compare up to four countries in estimated levels and trends of (a) inequality in disposable (post-tax, post-transfer) income, (b) inequality in market (pre-tax, pre-transfer) income, (c) absolute redistribution (market-income inequality minus net-income inequality), or (d) relative redistribution (market-income inequality minus net-income inequality, divided by market-income inequality), or to compare several of these measures in a single country, use the SWIID web app below. To use the SWIID in statistical analyses, datasets formatted for use in Stata and R are available for download.\n\n[__Explore the Source Data](swiid_source/) [__SWIID FAQ](swiid_faq/) [__Download the SWIID](swiid_downloads/)\n\nPowered by [RStudio](http://shiny.rstudio.com).\n\nPlease cite the SWIID as follows:\n\nSolt, Frederick. 2020. \u201cMeasuring Income Inequality Across Countries and Over Time: The Standardized World Income Inequality Database.\u201d _Social Science Quarterly_ 101(3):1183-1199. SWIID Version 9.92, April 2026.\n[Download BibTeX citation](../papers/solt2020/solt2020.bib).\n\n\nI gratefully acknowledge the support for the SWIID provided by the [U.S. National Science Foundation](https://www.nsf.gov/awardsearch/showAward?AWD_ID=1533746), the University of Konstanz [Cluster of Excellence Politics of Inequality](https://www.exc.uni-konstanz.de/en/inequality/), the [Department of Political Science](http://clas.uiowa.edu/polisci/) and [College of Liberal Arts and Sciences](http://clas.uiowa.edu/) at the University of Iowa, and the [Department of Political Science](https://academics.siu.edu/public-policy/political-science/) and [College of Liberal Arts](https://cola.siu.edu) of Southern Illinois University, Carbondale.\n\n  * [__Edit this page](https://github.com/fsolt/fsolt.github.io/edit/main/swiid/index.qmd)\n  * [ __Report an issue](https://github.com/fsolt/fsolt.github.io/issues/new)"}], "is_error": false, "command": "$PY \"$SKILL_DIR/scripts/aii_fast_web_fetch.py\" fetch --url \"https://fsolt.org/swiid/\" --max-chars 15000", "exit_code": 0, "timeout": false, "metadata": {"exit_code": 0, "pid": -1, "username": "adrian", "hostname": "vm", "working_dir": "/home/adrian/projects/ai-inventor/aii_data/users/admin/runs/run_-w6fuC_zXl2B/3_invention_loop/iter_1/gen_art/gen_art_research_1", "py_interpreter_path": "/home/adrian/projects/ai-inventor/.venv/bin/python", "prefix": "", "suffix": "\n[The command completed with exit code 0.]"}, "full_output_save_dir": "/home/adrian/projects/ai-inventor/aii_data/users/admin/runs/run_-w6fuC_zXl2B/.oh_sessions/8068fb54ebc9406087c7d39e5d398164/observations", "kind": "TerminalObservation"}

Corrections\n\n\n\n## Author\n\nListed:\n\n  * Frederick Solt\n\n\n\n[Registered:](https://authors.repec.org/)\n\n\n\n\n## Abstract\n\nObjective This article documents wide\u2010ranging revisions to the Standardized World Income Inequality Database (SWIID), which seeks to maximize the comparability of income inequality estimates for the broadest possible coverage of countries and years. Methods Two k\u2010fold cross\u2010validations, by observation and by country, are used to evaluate the SWIID's success in predicting the Luxembourg Income Study (LIS), recognized in the field as setting the standard for comparability. Results The cross\u2010validations indicate that the new SWIID's estimates and their uncertainty are even more accurate than previous versions, extending its advantage in comparability over alternate income inequality data sets. Conclusion Given its superior coverage and comparability, the SWIID remains the optimum source of data for broadly cross\u2010national research on income inequality.\n\n## Suggested Citation\n\n* Frederick Solt, 2020. \"**[Measuring Income Inequality Across Countries and Over Time: The Standardized World Income Inequality Database](/a/bla/socsci/v101y2020i3p1183-1199.html)** ,\" [Social Science Quarterly](/s/bla/socsci.html), Southwestern Social Science Association, vol. 101(3), pages 1183-1199, May.\nHandle: _RePEc:bla:socsci:v:101:y:2020:i:3:p:1183-1199_\nDOI: 10.1111/ssqu.12795\n\n__as HTML HTML with abstract plain text plain text with abstract BibTeX RIS (EndNote, RefMan, ProCite) ReDIF JSON\n\n## Download full text from publisher\n\n**File URL:** https://doi.org/10.1111/ssqu.12795\n**Download Restriction:** no\n\n\n* * *\n\n**File URL:** https://libkey.io/10.1111/ssqu.12795?utm_source=ideas\n**LibKey link** : if access is restricted and if your library uses this service, LibKey will redirect you to where you can use your library subscription to access this item\n\\---><\\---\n\n## References listed on IDEAS\n\n__as HTML HTML with abstract plain text plain text with abstract BibTeX RIS (EndNote, RefMan, ProCite) ReDIF JSON\n\n  1. Introduction\nEqual access to education is among the basic human rights to which everyone is entitled. Yet,\nthe educational gaps between various groups in many countries are staggering, as shown by\nmany studies. If people's abilities are normally distributed, then a skewed distribution of\neducation opportunities represents large welfare losses. As with land and machinery, an\nequitable distribution of human capital (basic literacy and nutrition/health) constitutes a\nprecondition for individual productivity and ability to rise above poverty.' Furthermore, an\nequitable distribution of opportunities is preferable to a redistribution of existing assets or\nincomes. This is because education builds new assets and improves social welfare by its spill-\nover effect, without making anyone worse off. Ensuring access to educational opportunities by\nattending to both the supply and demand sides, is a win-win policy gaining support in industrial\nand developing countries. To support such an effort, an indicator that can be easily calculated and\nmonitored over time would be useful.\nThis paper aims at developing a measure for educational inequality, using the concept of\neducation Gini index based on school attainment data, for a large number of countries over time.\nEducation Gini could be used as one of the indicators of welfare, complementing average\neducational attainment, health and nutrition, income per capita, and other indicators of welfare.\nAfter reviewing the literature and discussing the methodology, we look into the relationship\nbetween the education Gini index and average educational attainment, gender gaps, and the\nstandard deviation of education. As a narrowly focused technical paper, we do not attempt to\nfind a causal relationship between inequality in education and growth, as they could be jointly\ndetermined and mutually reinforcing.\nStandard deviations and Gini coefficients are often chosen as measures of inequality.\nStandard divisions of school attainment were used in a few studies. Only four previous studies\nwere found to have used Gini coefficients in measuring education inequality. They estimated the\nGini coefficient based on either enrollment or education finance. To properly measure education\ninequality, a Gini index should be based on educational attainment data. However, to our\nknowledge, no study has estimated education Gini coefficient based on educational attainment\nfor a large number of industrial and developing countries. (See table 1 for a selective literature\nreview.)\n2. Indicators to Measure Various Aspects of Education\nVarious indicators have been used to measure different aspects of education in cross country\nanalyses. These indicators include, among others, enrollment ratios, educational attainment,\nquality by input of resources, and quality by cognitive test scores. On the distribution of\n' There was a heated debate over the \"Equity of what?\" Amartya Sen (1980) sees individual's levels of functionings,\nsuch as literacy and nutrition, as attributes to be equalized. Others see the opportunities people face as the attributes\nto be equalized (Arneson 1989, Cohen 1989, and Roemer 1993). Yet others consider the amount of resources as the\nattribute to be equalized (Dworkin 1981).\n3\n\neducation, standard deviation of years of schooling was used, and lately, the education Gini\nindex, as a new indicator to measure education inequality. This section briefly explains the\nusefulness and problems of the various indicators that measure different aspects of education.\nFlow variable: Enrollment Ratios. At the early stage, the enrollment ratios for different\nlevels of schooling were used as indicators of human development (Barro 1991, Mankiw et al\n1992, Levine and Renelt 1992, Levine and Zervos 1993). The most commonly used are the\nprimary- and secondary enrollment ratios. One problem of this approach is that enrollment ratio\nonly measures the flow of population's education or access to education. It does not show the\ncumulated educational attainment/outcome. As a result, it is often inappropriate to use these\nenrollment ratios in growth models. Measuring education inequality based on enrollment data is\nalso problematic as they do not reflect the stock of human capital.\nStock variable: Attainment measured by Number of Average Years of Schooling.\nPsacharopoulos and Arriagada (1986) suggested that the proper indicator of human development\nlevel is the stock of educational attainment defined as average years of schooling.\nPsacharopoulos and Arriagada (1986) made the efforts of collecting the census information on\neach country's schooling distribution over the entire population, and calculated educational\nattainment. Barro and Lee (1991, 1993, and 1997) gathered more data and formalized the use of\neducational attainment for growth regressions. Nehru, Swanson, and Dubey (1994) also created a\ncross country database for educational attainment, through estimating the schooling distributions\nover time for various countries. Measuring the distribution of education based on these\nattainment data becomes feasible.\nThe Quality of Schooling.\nEducational attainment across countries may not be\ncomparable as the quality of schooling differ widely. Behrman and Birdsall (1983) and others\n(Lockheed and Verspoor 1991; Card and Krueger 1992) warned that quantity alone is not\nenough, quality must be taken into consideration when measuring the level of human\ndevelopment. Two typical approaches were used to measure the quality of education, the input\napproach and the output approach, each with its own problems and limitations.\nThe Input Approach (Resources for Schooling). One way to measure the education\nquality is to see which country devotes more resources to education than others. Resources being\ninputted into the education systems can be measured by pupil-teacher ratio, by expenditures on\nteachers' wage, by spending on book and reading materials. One problem is that high volume of\ninput does not necessarily yield high quality. Another problem is that the inputs for schools are\nnot independent of the income. There is limited feasibility of using inputs of schooling as proxies\nfor education quality (Hanushek and Kim 1995).\nThe Output Approach (Test Score of Cognitive Performance). The output approach\ndirectly measures the achievements of schooling by comparing the scores of cognitive\nperformance, which the students of the same-age group of various countries obtained through the\nsame international tests on the same subjects including mathematics and science. The tests to\nassess student achievement in mathematics and science were conducted both by the International\nAssociation for Evaluation of Educational Achievement (IEA) and by international Assessment\n4\n\nof Fiducation progress (1EAP).2 Two problems that prevented these measures to be widely used\ninclude that first, they are only available for a dozen, mostly industrial, countries, and second,\nthey are not comparable over time. It is for these reasons, we cannot use them to control for the\nquality of education in the education Gini index that we constructed.\nThe Distribution of Education: why important. The distributional dimension of education\nis extremely important for both welfare consideration and for production. If an asset, say physical\ncapital, is freely traded across firms in a competitive environment, its marginal product will be\nequalized through free-market mechanism. As a result, its contribution to output will not be\naffected by its distribution across firms or individuals. If an asset is not completely tradable,\nhowever, then the marginal product of the asset across individuals is not equalized, and there is\nan aggregation problem. In this case, aggregate production finction depends not only on the\naverage level of the asset but also on its distribution. Because education/skill is only partially\ntradable, the average level of educational attainment alone is not sufficient to reflect the\ncharacteristics of a country's human capital. We need to look beyond averages and investigate\nboth the absolute dispersion and the relative dispersion of human capital.\nStandard Deviation of Schooling: Absolute Dispersion. There is a small but growing\nliterature on schooling inequality or the distribution of education (see, for example, Lam and\nLevinson 1991; Londofio 1990; Ram 1990). As data became available for measuring the\ndistribution of education, the disparities became more apparent. Standard deviations have been\nused often to measure the absolute dispersion distribution of assets. Birdsall and Londono\n(1997), investigating the impact of initial asset distribution on growth and poverty reduction,\nfound a significant negative correlation between education dispersion (measured by the standard\ndeviation of schooling) and income growth. In the Inter-American Development Bank (1999)\nstudy on inequality in Latin America, the standard deviation of schooling is used to measure\ninequality of education, and it was found that the larger the standard deviation of schooling, the\ngreater the income inequality--measured by income Gini. (See Table 1 for literature review). Rati\nRam used the standard deviations of schooling to illustrate the existence of an education Kuznuts\ncurve, and concluded that, \"As the average level of schooling rises, educational inequality first\nincreases, and after reaching a peak, starts to decline. The tuming point is about seven years of\neducation\" (Ram 1990). Londofno 1990 also used the same method.\n2 Hanushek and Kim (1995) first transformed the test scores into unified scales, and then utilized the index to test\nhow much of the growth was affected by both the quantity and the quality of education. They found that both the\nquantity and the quality of schooling positively contribute to the growth of income, at statistically significant levels.\nThe output approach measures the quality of education based on student cognitive performance, which was\nregarded by Hanushek and Kim (1995) as a complement to the quantity of education developed by Barro and Lee\n(1993). Lee and Barro (1997) investigated the determinants of school quality, and found that greater school inputs,\nlonger school terms, family background, and strong communities are positively related to student performance.\nHowever, they cannot fully explain the better education outcomes in East Asian countries than in other developing\ncountries. It suggests other factors at play, including those associated with a more open and export-oriented\neconomic environment.\n5\n\nEducation Gini: Measurement of Relative Inequality. Standard deviation of schooling\nonly measures the dispersion of schooling distribution in absolute terms. Education Gini, which are similar to the Gini coefficients widely used to\nmeasure distributions of income, wealth, and land, ranges from 0, which represents perfect\nequality, to 1, which represents perfect inequality. Education Gini coefficients can be calculated\nusing enrollment, financing, or attainment data. Maas and Criel (1982) estimated Gini\ncoefficients based on enrollment data for 16 East African countries. {"content": [{"cache_prompt": false, "type": "text", "text": "URL: https://ourworldindata.org/owid-data-collection-inequality-and-poverty\nType: HTML\nLength: 24201 chars (truncated)\n\n--- Content ---\n\n[Our World\nin Data](/)\n\n[](https://www.oxfordmartin.ox.ac.uk/global-development)[](https://www.ox.ac.uk/)[](https://global-change-data-lab.org/)\n\n  * Browse by topic\n\n  * [Data](/search)\n  * [Latest](/latest)\n  * Resources\n\n  * About\n\n\n\n\nSubscribe\n\n[Donate](/donate)\n\n[Home](https://ourworldindata.org/)[Economic Inequality](https://ourworldindata.org/economic-inequality)\n\n# OWID Data Collection: Inequality and Poverty\n\n## Explore a wide range of indicators on inequality and poverty and compare sources.\n\nBy [Joe Hasell](/team/joe-hasell) and [Pablo Arriagada](/team/pablo-arriagada)\n\nJuly 6, 2023\n\nBrowse past versions\n\nCite this articleReuse our work freely\n\nAt Our World in Data, we are building an extensive dataset of inequality and poverty indicators, pulling together multiple sources to provide as comprehensive a view as possible.\n\nTo make it easier to navigate this wide range of data, below we provide links to a set of Data Explorers that allow you to explore a very detailed range of indicators and compare data across sources. The explorers draw from three prominent sources, each offering a global perspective on poverty and inequality: the [World Bank Poverty and Inequality Platform](https://pip.worldbank.org/home), the [Luxembourg Income Study](https://www.lisdatacenter.org/), and the [World Inequality Database](https://wid.world/). Information about the definitions and methods behind the data from each of these sources is provided at the bottom of this page.\n\nThe detailed data contained in the explorers collected below is intended for experts or researchers who are already quite familiar with the measures and concepts involved. Users with a more general interest are likely to benefit more from the Data Explorers shown in our topic pages on [Inequality](https://ourworldindata.org/economic-inequality#explore-data-on-economic-inequality) and [Poverty](https://ourworldindata.org/poverty#explore-data-on-poverty). These provide an overview of the key indicators from this collection.\n\n* * *\n\n## Poverty indicators:\n\n  * [Poverty Data Explorer: World Bank PIP data](https://ourworldindata.org/explorers/poverty-wb)\n  * [Poverty Data Explorer: Luxembourg Income Study data](https://ourworldindata.org/explorers/poverty-lis)\n\n\n\n## Inequality indicators:\n\n  * [Inequality Data Explorer: World Bank PIP data](https://ourworldindata.org/explorers/inequality-wb)\n  * [Inequality Data Explorer: Luxembourg Income Study data](https://ourworldindata.org/explorers/inequality-lis)\n  * [Inequality Data Explorer: World Inequality Database data](https://ourworldindata.org/explorers/inequality-wid)\n\n\n\n## Incomes across the distribution:\n\n  * [Incomes Across the Distribution Data Explorer: World Bank data](https://ourworldindata.org/explorers/incomes-across-distribution-wb)\n  * [Incomes Across the Distribution Data Explorer: Luxembourg Income Study data](https://ourworldindata.org/explorers/incomes-across-distribution-lis)\n  * [Incomes Across the Distribution Data Explorer: World Inequality Database data](https://ourworldindata.org/explorers/incomes-across-distribution-wid)\n\n\n\n* * *\n\n##### Subscribe to our newsletters\n\nWe send two regular newsletters so you can stay up to date on our work and receive curated highlights from across Our World in Data.\n\n[Subscribe](/subscribe)\n\n# Details on the methods used by each data source\n\n## World Bank PIP\n\nThe [World Bank Poverty and Inequality Platform](https://pip.worldbank.org/home) is an interactive website and API that the World Bank uses to share the estimates it produces in its activities of monitoring global poverty, inequality and shared prosperity.\n\nHere we summarize some key aspects of the definitions and methods used in the platform\u2019s data.\n\nFor a more detailed discussion, see the World Bank PIP [methodology document](https://datanalytics.worldbank.org/PIP-Methodology/).\n\n**Welfare measure**\n\nThe data collated in the PIP data relates to a mix of after-tax income and consumption, depending on the country and year. While in most high-income countries, the data relates to after-tax income, in poorer countries, the data tends to relate to consumption.\n\nThe World Bank pools the data to get a global picture of poverty and inequality. But it\u2019s essential to remember that, depending on the country or year, somewhat different things are being measured.\n\nIn the Data Explorers of the World Bank data above, we provide the option of plotting these after-tax income and consumption data points separately.\n\nThe World Bank PIP data provides no indicators in terms of before-tax income.\n\nTo make absolute comparisons of living standards across countries and over time, the World Bank converts the survey data \u2013 measured in local currencies at current prices \u2013 into constant international dollars. The World Bank data shown above is all measured in 2021 international dollars.\n\n**Primary data sources**\n\nThe World Bank PIP estimates are derived from a large collection of household surveys.\n\nIn addition to the difference between income and consumption data mentioned above, there are several other ways in which comparability across household surveys can be limited, both across countries and over time. In collating this survey data, the World Bank takes various steps to harmonize it where possible, but comparability issues remain. The PIP [Methodology Handbook](https://datanalytics.worldbank.org/PIP-Methodology/) provides a good summary of the comparability and data quality issues affecting this data and how it tries to address them.\n\nTo help communicate this limitation of the data, the World Bank produces a companion indicator that groups data points within each country into \u2018spells\u2019. The surveys underlying the data within a given spell for a particular country are considered by World Bank researchers to be more comparable. In the Data Explorers of the World Bank data above, we provide the option of plotting the data with the breaks between spells shown.\n\n**Accounting for resource sharing within households**\n\nThe surveys on which the World Bank estimates are conducted at the _household_ level. The income or consumption reported in the survey data sums across all household members.\n\nIn calculating its poverty and inequality indicators, the World Bank uses _per capita_ income or consumption: it attributes an equal share of household income to each member \u2013 adults and children.\n\n**Methods and assumptions applied**\n\nIn some cases, the raw household survey data itself is not made available to the World Bank. In these cases, their estimates are based on \u2018grouped data\u2019 \u2013 tabulations of the average incomes of richer and poorer segments of the population. To produce its poverty and inequality estimates, the World Bank fits a distribution to this grouped data, by making certain assumptions about the shape of that distribution.\n\nAs we discuss more in [this article](https://ourworldindata.org/how-has-income-inequality-within-countries-evolved-over-the-past-century), a well-known issue with household survey data is that the incomes of the richest are often poorly captured. This can lead to underestimates of inequality. Statistical offices organizing household surveys may adopt various strategies to minimize this, but this varies across countries and over time. In processing this survey data, the World Bank takes no steps to further correct the problem of missing top incomes. As such, inequality indicators based on this data \u2013 particularly those sensitive to the top, such as top income shares \u2013 may, in many cases, underestimate inequality.\n\n## Luxembourg Income Study\n\nThe [Luxembourg Income Study](https://www.lisdatacenter.org/) (LIS) is a collection of household survey data, in which the raw data produced by different statistical offices is reorganized to make them more comparable. One particular benefit of LIS is that it provides access to the \u2018microdata\u2019 \u2013 the data for particular individuals and households participating in the survey.\n\nOur World in Data processes a range of poverty and inequality indicators, provided by LIS.\n\n**Welfare measure**\n\nWe show poverty and inequality indicators for both after-tax and before-tax income. The definitions for these indicators align with those used in LIS\u2019 [DART](https://dart.lisdatacenter.org/dart) data visualization tool and their [Key Figures](https://www.lisdatacenter.org/lis-ikf-webapp/app/search-ikf-figures) estimates, described [here](https://www.lisdatacenter.org/data-access/dart/methodology/).\n\nAs a measure of after-tax income, we use their measure of \u2018disposable household income\u2019. This refers to \u201ccash and non-cash income from labor, income from capital, income from pensions (including private and public pensions) and non-pension public social benefits stemming from insurance, universal or assistance schemes (including in-kind social assistance transfers), as well as cash and non-cash private transfers, after deduction of the amount of income taxes and social contributions paid\u201d.\n\nAs a measure of before-tax income we use their measure of \u2018market income\u2019. This refers to \u201cincome received by the households before public redistribution takes place; it includes cash and non-cash income from labor, income from capital, income from private pensions, as well as cash and non-cash private transfers, before deduction of income taxes and social contributions paid\u201d.1\n\nIn order to make absolute comparisons of standards of living across countries and over time, the data is converted into constant international dollars (from local currencies at current prices coming from the surveys). The LIS data shown above is all measured in 2021 international dollars.\n\n**Primary data sources**\n\nThe LIS data is a \u2018harmonized\u2019 collection of household survey data. This means that the raw data produced by different statistical offices has been reorganized to align the concepts behind the data as much as possible.\n\nThe underlying survey data is, however, very heterogeneous, and not all comparability issues can be resolved. To communicate these issues, LIS has released the very helpful [Compare.it](https://www.lisdatacenter.org/our-data/survey-comparability-tool/) tool, which provides very detailed comparability notes for each country.\n\n**Accounting for resource sharing within households**\n\nThe surveys LIS collates are conducted at the _household_ level. The income or consumption reported in the survey data sums across all members of the household.\n\nFrom the LIS microdata, we show poverty and inequality indicators based on two approaches for accounting for resource sharing within households:\n\n  * **_Per capita_****income:** here, each member of the household (both adults and children) is attributed an income equal to total household income divided by the number of household members.\n  * **_Equivalized_****income:** on this basis, incomes are adjusted to account for the fact that people in the same household can share costs like rent and heating. We use the \u2018square root\u2019 equivalence scale to make this adjustment: each household member (both adults and children) is attributed an income equal to the total household income divided by the _square root_ of the number of household members.\n\n\n\n**Methods and assumptions applied**\n\nLIS provides very detailed documentation of how they process the original survey data on two dedicated metadata platforms: [METIS](https://www.lisdatacenter.org/frontend#/home) and the [Compare.it](https://www.lisdatacenter.org/our-data/survey-comparability-tool/) tool.\n\nIn calculating inequality and poverty estimates from the microdata, LIS applies \u2018top-\u2019 and \u2018bottom-coding\u2019 procedures to calculate their summary statistics presented on their website \u2013 both the LIS \u2018Key Figures\u2019 and the DART interactive visualization tool. This is done to remove extreme values from the raw survey data and to make the data across countries more comparable. For a more detailed discussion of why this is done, the methods used, and how it impacts resulting estimates, see the helpful [explainer from LIS](https://www.lisdatacenter.org/newsletter/nl-2020-15-im-4/).\n\nA well-known issue with household survey data is that the incomes of the richest are often poorly captured. This can lead to underestimates of inequality. Statistical offices organizing household surveys may adopt various strategies to minimize this, but this varies across countries and over time. In processing this survey data, the Luxembourg Income Study itself takes no steps to try to further correct the problem of missing top incomes. As such, inequality indicators based on this data \u2013 particularly those sensitive to the top, such as top income shares \u2013 may, in many cases, underestimate inequality.\n\n## World Inequality Database\n\nThe [World Inequality Database](https://wid.world/) (WID) is an extensive database on the distribution of income and wealth maintained by the World Inequality Lab (WIL), located at the Paris School of Economics (PSE). The database is the result of a collaborative effort involving many researchers worldwide.\n\n**Primary data sources, welfare measures, and methods**\n\nA distinctive feature of the WID data is the broad range of raw data sources it draws on.\n\nMost sources of inequality data draw exclusively on household surveys. As we discuss more in [this article](https://ourworldindata.org/how-has-income-inequality-within-countries-evolved-over-the-past-century), A downside of this approach is that the incomes of the richest are often poorly captured in survey data. This can lead to underestimates of inequality, particularly for measures focused on the top of the distribution, such as the share of income of the richest 1%.\n\nThe WID database emerged from the substantial literature on \u2018top incomes\u2019 that sought to address this shortcoming of survey data by relying instead on data obtained from tax records, or tabulations of such data released by tax authorities. The use of such tax data often limited what concept of income could be analyzed. Inequality estimates produced within the \u2018top incomes\u2019 literature have generally been measured in terms of before-tax income, with the exact definitions varying due to differences in the tax system across countries or over time. Since, in many places or periods, it is only a relatively small population of high-earning individuals that file tax returns, the use of tax data also required a focus on the top of the income distribution \u2013 for example, on the share of income received by the top 1 or 10%.2\n\nThese methodologies have continued to develop, and the WID database has established a more standardized set of methods. Within this approach, tax data is combined with data from household surveys and national accounts to produce [Distributional National Accounts](https://wid.world/document/distributional-national-accounts-guidelines-2020-concepts-and-methods-used-in-the-world-inequality-database/) (DINA). But when\nit is led by a reasonably enlightened group of people, as China is today, it\ncan also have great advantages. That one party can just impose the polit-\nically dif\ufb01cult but critically important policies needed to move a society\nforward in the 21st century.\u201d Robert Barro (1997, 1) states this view even\nmore boldly: \u201cMore political rights do not have an effect on growth.\u201d\nAlthough some recent contributions estimate a positive effect of de-\nmocracy on growth, the pessimistic view of the economic implications\nof democracy is still widely shared. From their review of the academic lit-\nerature until the mid-2000s, Gerring et al. (2005, 323) conclude that \u201cthe\nnet effect of democracy on growth performance cross-nationally over the\nlast \ufb01ve decades is negative or null.\u201d\nIn this paper, we challenge this view. Using a panel of countries from\n1960 to 2010, we estimate the impact on economic growth of the unprec-\nedented spread of democracy around the world that took place in the\npast 50 years. The evidence suggests that democracy does cause growth\nand that its effect is signi\ufb01cant and sizable.1 Our estimates imply that a\ncountry that transitions from nondemocracy to democracy achieves\nabout 20 percent higher GDP per capita in the next 25 years than a coun-\ntry that remains a nondemocracy. The effect of democracy does not de-\npend on the initial level of economic development, although we \ufb01nd\nsome evidence that democracy is more conducive to growth in countries\nwith greater levels of secondary education.\nThe estimation of the causal effect of democracy (or a democratiza-\ntion) on GDP faces several challenges. First, existing democracy indices\n1 Our speci\ufb01cations focus on the effect of democracy on the level of log GDP per capita,\nso that democratization affects growth in log GDP per capita. With some abuse of terminol-\nogy, we sometimes describe this as \u201cthe impact of democracy on economic growth\u201d (rather\nthan the impact of democratization on economic growth) or \u201cthe impact of democracy on\nGDP.\u201d For brevity, as in the last expression, we also often refer to GDP instead of GDP per\ncapita.\n48\njournal of political economy\n\nare subject to considerable measurement error, leading to spurious\nchanges in democracy scores that do not correspond to real changes in\ndemocratic institutions.\nSecond, democracies differ from nondemocracies in unobserved char-\nacteristics, such as institutional, historical, and cultural aspects, that also\nhave an impact on their GDP. As a result, cross-country regressions, as\nthose in Barro (1996, 1999), could be biased and are unlikely to reveal\nthe causal effect of democracy on growth. Recent studies tackle this prob-\nlem by using difference-in-differences or panel data estimates with coun-\ntry \ufb01xed effects.\nThird, as shown in \ufb01gure 1, as well as in Acemoglu et al. (2005) and\nBr\u00fcckner and Ciccone (2011), democratizations are, on average, pre-\nceded by a temporary dip in GDP. This \ufb01gure depicts GDP dynamics in\ncountries that democratized at year 0 relative to other countries that re-\nmained nondemocratic at the time.
