Though largely overlooked in previous work, the dip in GDP that\nFIG. 1.\u2014GDP per capita before and after a democratization. This \ufb01gure plots GDP per\ncapita in log points around a democratic transition relative to countries remaining non-\ndemocratic in the same year. We normalize log GDP per capita to 0 in the year preceding\nthe democratization. Time (in years) relative to the year of democratization runs on the\nhorizontal axis.\ndemocracy does cause growth\n49\n\nprecedes a democratization constitutes a clear violation of the parallel-\ntrends assumption that underlies the difference-in-differences or panel\ndata estimates used in the literature. Modeling GDP dynamics would also\nenable an investigation of whether the impact of democratization on\nGDP is short-lived or gradual.\nLast but not least, even if we control for country \ufb01xed effects and GDP\ndynamics, changes in democracy could be driven by time-varying un-\nobservables related to future economic conditions, potentially leading\nto biased estimates.\nIn this paper, we address these challenges. We build on the important\nwork by Papaioannou and Siourounis (2008) to develop a dichotomous\nmeasure of democracy, which combines several indices to purge spurious\nchanges in each. We rely on this measure for most of our analysis but also\ndocument the robustness of our results to other measures of democracy\nin the online appendix.\nThere is no perfect strategy for tackling the remaining challenges and\nestimating the causal effect of democracy on GDP. Our approach is to use\na number of different strategies, which reassuringly all give similar re-\nsults. Our \ufb01rst approach uses a dynamic (linear) panel model for GDP,\nwhich includes both country \ufb01xed effects and autoregressive dynamics.\nThe underlying economic assumption here is that, conditional on the lags\nof GDP and country \ufb01xed effects, countries that change their democratic\nstatus are not on a differential GDP trend (and thus these lags successfully\nmodel the dip in GDP that precedes democratizations shown in \ufb01g. 1).\nThis strategy leads to robust and precise estimates that indicate that in\nthe 25 years following a permanent democratization, GDP per capita is\nabout 20 percent higher than it would be otherwise.\nOur second strategy adopts a semiparametric treatment effects frame-\nwork in which democratization\u2014the treatment\u2014in\ufb02uences the distribu-\ntion of potential GDP in all subsequent years. This strategy requires us to\nmodel the process of selection into democracy as a function of observ-\nables, in particular, lags of GDP (e.g., Jord\u00e0 2005; Angrist and Kuersteiner\n2011; Kline 2011), but it does not rely on a parametric model for the dy-\nnamics of GDP, which affords us greater \ufb02exibility in estimating the time\npath of the impact of democracy on GDP. Related to our \ufb01rst approach,\nthe economic assumption in this case is that, conditional on the lags of\nGDP, countries that democratize are not on a differential GDP trend rel-\native to other nondemocracies. We show that this approach successfully\ncontrols for the in\ufb02uence of the dip in GDP preceding democratizations\nshown in \ufb01gure 1 and estimates that after a democratization, GDP in-\ncreases gradually until it reaches a level 20\u201325 percent higher than what\nit would reach otherwise.\nThese two strategies model the selection of countries into different re-\ngimes and control for the dip in GDP in \ufb01gure 1 as a function of their re-\n50\njournal of political economy\n\ncent GDP per capita and time-invariant unobserved heterogeneity. How-\never, they do not tackle the possibility that both democracy and GDP\nmight be affected by time-varying omitted variables. Our third strategy\nconfronts this challenge by using an instrumental-variables (IV) approach.\nThe political science literature emphasizes that transitions to democracy\noften take place in regional waves (e.g., Huntington 1991; Markoff 1996).\nOn the basis of this observation, we use regional waves in transitions to\nand away from democracy as an instrument for country-level democracy.\nOur IV strategy exploits the diffusion of political regimes across countries\nin the same region and with common political histories. We pay special at-\ntention to distinguishing the diffusion of democracy from the role of re-\ngional economic shocks or the spread of economic conditions to nearby\ncountries through trade and other mechanisms. By focusing on the varia-\ntion created by regional waves of democratizations, our IV strategy ensures\nthat idiosyncratic changes in a country\u2019s political regime that may be en-\ndogenous to its growth do not bias our estimates. The resulting estimates\nof the impact of democracy on GDP are similar to those from our other two\nstrategies: in our preferred speci\ufb01cation, a democratization increases GDP\nper capita by about 25 percent in the \ufb01rst 25 years\u2014although in some spec-\ni\ufb01cations the estimated effects are larger. This similarity bolsters our con-\n\ufb01dence that all three of our strategies are estimating the causal effect of\ndemocracy on GDP.\nWe further investigate the channels through which democracy increases\nGDP. Although our \ufb01ndings here are less clear-cut than our baseline re-\nsults, they suggest that democracy contributes to growth by increasing\ninvestment, encouraging economic reforms, improving the provision of\nschooling and health care, and reducing social unrest. These results are\nconsistent with, though of course do not prove, the hypothesis that democ-\nracies invest more in broad-based public goods and are more likely to enact\neconomic reforms that would otherwise be resisted by politically powerful\nactors (e.g., Acemoglu 2008). Although nondemocracies could also invest\nin public goods or enact far-ranging economic reforms, our results indi-\ncate that, at least in our sample, these countries are less likely to do so than\ndemocracies.\nAt the end of the paper, we turn to the common claim that democracy\nconstrains economic growth for countries with low levels of development\n(e.g., Aghion, Alesina, and Trebbi 2008; Posner 2010; Brooks 2013). Our\nresults do not support this view, but we do \ufb01nd that democracy has a larger\nimpact on growth in countries where a greater fraction of the population\nhas secondary schooling.\nThere is a substantial literature in political science that investigates, but\ndoes not reach a \ufb01rm conclusion on, the empirical linkages between democ-\nracy and economic outcomes, summarized in part in Przeworski and Li-\nmongi (1993) and in Doucouliagos and Uluba\u015fo\u011flu\u2019s (2008) meta-analysis.\ndemocracy does cause growth\n51\n\nCross-country regression analyses, such as Helliwell (1994), Barro (1996,\n1999), and Tavares and Wacziarg (2001), have produced negative, though\ngenerally inconsistent, results.2 More recent work, including Rodrik and\nWacziarg (2005), Persson and Tabellini (2006), Papaioannou and Siou-\nrounis (2008), and Bates, Fayad, and Hoef\ufb02er (2012), estimate positive ef-\nfects using panel data techniques, although Murtin and Wacziarg (2014),\nBurkhart and Lewis-Beck (1994), and Giavazzi and Tabellini (2005) esti-\nmate insigni\ufb01cant effects on growth using similar strategies.3 These and\nother papers in this literature differ in their measure of democracy and\nchoice of speci\ufb01cations and neither systematically control for the dynam-\nics of GDP nor address the endogeneity of democratizations. Although\nsome of the papers in this literature control for lags of GDP in some of\ntheir speci\ufb01cations (e.g., Persson and Tabellini 2006; Papaioannou and\nSiourounis 2008; Murtin and Wacziarg 2014), they do not emphasize the\nimportance of GDP dynamics and the bias that results from not appro-\npriately controlling for the dip in GDP shown in \ufb01gure 1. The failure to rec-\nognize this point may, in fact, explain the divergent results in the literature:\nbecause growth rates are less serially correlated than GDP, contributions\nthat focus on growth as the dependent variable tend to \ufb01nd positive effects,\nwhile studies that estimate models in levels generally \ufb01nd no effects\u2014un-\nless they model the dynamics of GDP as we do.\nPersson and Tabellini (2008), too, use propensity score techniques to\nestimate the impact of democracy. However, they focus only on changes\nin the average growth rate of countries after a democratization and do\nnot develop the semiparametric approach we use here or model the se-\nlection into democracy as a function of lags of GDP. Also related is recent\nindependent work by Meyersson (2015), who estimates the effect of suc-\ncessful coups on economic growth by comparing them to unsuccessful\ncoups.\n2 Another related literature investigates the effect of economic growth on democracy\n(e.g., Lipset 1959). We do not focus on this relationship here, although \ufb01g. 1 clearly im-\nplies a very different pattern: temporary drops in GDP make transitions to democracy more\nlikely. In the online appendix, we also con\ufb01rm that, consistent with Acemoglu et al. (2008,\n2009), the level of GDP has no effect on democratizations, but it does have some impact on\ntransitions to nondemocracy.\n3 A smaller literature focuseson the effects of democracyon other economic outcomes. For\nexample, Grosjean and Senik (2011), Rode and Gwartney (2012), and Giuliano, Mishra, and\nSpilimbergo (2013) look at the effect of democracy on economic reforms. Ansell (2010) looks\nat its impact on educational spending. Gerring, Thacker, and Alfaro (2012), Blaydes and\nKayser (2011), Besley and Kudamatsu (2006), and Kudamatsu (2012) investigate its impact\non health, infant mortality, and nutrition outcomes. Reynal-Querol (2005) and Sunde and\nCervellati (2014) study its impact on civil war. A more sizable literature looks at the effects\nof democracy on redistribution and inequality and is reviewed and extended in Acemoglu\net al. (2015). There is also a growing and promising literature that investigates the impact\nof democracy by using within-country differences in the extent of democratic and electoral\ninstitutions (see, among others, Martinez-Bravo et al. 2012; Naidu 2012; Fujiwara 2015).\n52\njournal of political economy\n\nWe also build on and complement Persson and Tabellini (2009), who\nexploit variation in geographically proximate neighbors\u2019 democracy (or,\nmore precisely, an inverse distance-weighted average of democracy among\n\u201cneighbors\u201d; see further Ansell 2010; Aidt and Jensen 2012; Madsen,\nRaschky, and Skali 2015). Using this approach, Persson and Tabellini es-\ntimate the impact of a country\u2019s \u201cdemocratic capital\u201d on growth. Unlike\nus, they do not instrument for democracy by using regional waves but use\nthe distance-weighted average of democracy among \u201cneighbors\u201d to con-\ntrol for the transitions into and out of democracy in a regression that fo-\ncuses on the impact on growth of a country\u2019s historical experience with\ndemocracy. Besides differences in question and speci\ufb01cation, our IV strat-\negy differs from theirs in that we focus on regional waves of democratiza-\ntion for countries with common political histories. We document below\nthat regional waves have much greater and more robust explanatory\npower on the likelihood of democracy for a given country than variation\ncoming from proximate neighbors\u2019 democracy.\nThe rest of the paper is organized as follows. The next section describes\nthe construction of our democracy index and provides data sources and\ndescriptive statistics for our sample. Section III presents our dynamic panel\nmodel results. This model is estimated with the standard within estimator\nand various generalized method of moments (GMM) estimators. This\nsection also presents a variety of robustness checks. Section IV introduces\nthe treatment effects framework and presents results from our semipara-\nmetric strategy. Section V presents our results obtained by instrumenting\ndemocracy with regional democratization waves. Section VI presents evi-\ndence on potential channels through which democracy affects growth.\nSection VII investigates heterogeneous effects of democracy depending\non the level of economic development and education. Section VIII con-\ncludes the paper. We present several additional exercises in our online ap-\npendix.\nII.\nData and Descriptive Statistics\nWe construct an annual panel that comprises 175 countries from 1960 to\n2010, although not all variables are available for the entire sample. Follow-\ning Papaioannou and Siourounis (2008), our index combines infor-\nmation from several data sets, including Freedom House and Polity IV,\nand considers a country democratic only when several sources classify it\nas such. First, we con-\nsider a country democratic during a given year if Freedom House codes it\ndemocracy does cause growth\n53\n\nas \u201cfree\u201d or \u201cpartially free\u201d and Polity IV assigns it a positive score. When\none of these two sources is unavailable, we verify whether the country is\nalso coded as democratic by Cheibub, Gandhi, and Vreeland (2010) or\nBoix, Miller, and Rosato (2012). Many of\nthe democratic transitions detected in this manner are studied in detail\nby Papaioannou and Siourounis (2008), who use historical sources to\ndate the exact year of the transition. When possible, we also draw on their\ndata to verify the date of a democratization event.\nOur measure of democracy covers 184 countries from 1960 to 2010 and\nis available for all the years during which a country was independent.4\nIn 1960, 31.5 percent of the countries in our sample were democracies.\nBy 2010, this percentage had increased to 64.1 percent, which shows\nthe unprecedented spread of democracy we study in this paper. The countries and years in which these events took\nplace are listed in online appendix tables A1 and A2. Then it shows\nhow limited time span and the potential for fixed effects and endogenous regressors drive the design\nof the estimators of interest, offering Stata-based examples along the way. Next it shows how to apply\nthese estimators with xtabond2. And I thank all the users whose\nfeedback has led to steady improvement in xtabond2. Address for correspondence: droodman@cgdev.org\n\n\nForeword\n\nThe Center for Global Development builds its policy recommendations on both\ntheoretical and empirical analysis. The empirical analysis often draws on historical, non-\nexperimental data, drawing inferences that rely partly on good judgment and partly on\nthe considered application of the most sophisticated and appropriate econometric\ntechniques available. This paper provides an introduction to econometric techniques that\nare specifically designed to extract causal lessons from data on a large number of\nindividuals (whether countries, firms, or people) each of which is observed only a few\ntimes, such as annually over five or ten years. These techniques were developed in the\n1990s by authors such as Manuel Arellano, Richard Blundell, and Olympia Bover, and\nhave been widely applied to estimate every thing from the impact of foreign aid to the\nimportance of financial sector development to the effects of AIDS deaths on households.\n\nThe present paper contributes to this literature pedagogically, by providing an original\nsynthesis and exposition of the literature on these \u201cdynamic panel estimators,\u201d and\npractically, by presenting the first implementation of some of these techniques in Stata, a\nstatistical software package widely used in the research community. Stata is designed to\nencourage users to develop new commands for it, which other users can then use or\neven modify. David Roodman\u2019s xtabond2, introduced here, is now one of the most\nfrequently downloaded user-written Stata commands in the world. Stata\u2019s partially open-\nsource architecture has encouraged the growth of a vibrant world-wide community of\nresearchers, which benefits not only from improvements made to Stata by the parent\ncorporation, but also from the voluntary contributions of other users. Stata is arguably\none of the best examples of a combination of private for-profit incentives and voluntary\nopen-source incentives in the joint creation of a global public good. The Center for\nGlobal Development is pleased to contribute this paper and two commands, called\nxtabond2 and abar, to the research community.\n\n\nNancy Birdsall\nPresident\nCenter for Global Development\n\n\nAbstract\nThe \u201cdi\ufb00erence\u201d and \u201csystem\u201d generalized method of moments (GMM) estimators, developed by Holtz-\nEakin, Newey, and Rosen (1988), Arellano and Bond (1991), Arellano and Bover (1995), and Blundell and\nBond (1998), are increasingly popular.\nBoth are general estimators designed for situations with \u201csmall\nT, large N\u201d panels, meaning few time periods and many individuals; with independent variables that are\nnot strictly exogenous, meaning correlated with past and possibly current realizations of the error; with\n\ufb01xed e\ufb00ects; and with heteroskedasticity and autocorrelation within individuals.\nThis pedagogic paper\n\ufb01rst introduces linear GMM. Then it shows how limited time span and the potential for \ufb01xed e\ufb00ects and\nendogenous regressors drive the design of the estimators of interest, o\ufb00ering Stata-based examples along\nthe way. Next it shows how to apply these estimators with xtabond2. It also explains how to perform the\nArellano-Bond test for autocorrelation in a panel after other Stata commands, using abar. The paper closes\nwith some tips for proper use.\n\n1\nIntroduction\nThe Arellano-Bond (1991) and Arellano-Bover (1995)/Blundell-Bond (1998) dynamic panel estimators are\nincreasingly popular. Both are general estimators designed for situations with 1) \u201csmall T, large N\u201d panels,\nmeaning few time periods and many individuals; 2) a linear functional relationship; 3) a single left-hand-side\nvariable that is dynamic, depending on its own past realizations; 4) independent variables that are not strictly\nexogneous, meaning correlated with past and possibly current realizations of the error; 5) \ufb01xed individual\ne\ufb00ects; and 6) heteroskedasticity and autocorrelation within individuals, but not across them. Arellano-\nBond estimation starts by transforming all regressors, usually by di\ufb00erencing, and uses the Generalized\nMethod of Moments (Hansen 1982), and so is called \u201cdi\ufb00erence GMM.\u201dfootnoteAs we will discuss, the\nforward orthogonal deviations transform, proposed by Arellano and Bover (1995), is sometimes performed\ninstead of di\ufb00erencing. The Arellano-Bover/Blundell-Bond estimator augments Arellano-Bond by making an\nadditional assumption, that \ufb01rst di\ufb00erences of instrumenting variables are uncorrelated with the \ufb01xed e\ufb00ects.\nThis allows the introduction of more instruments, and can dramatically improve e\ufb03ciency. It builds a system\nof two equations\u2014the original equation as well as the transformed one\u2014and is known as \u201csystem GMM.\u201d\nThe program xtabond2 implements these estimators. It has some important advantages over Stata\u2019s built-in\nxtabond. It implements system GMM. It can make the Windmeijer (2005) \ufb01nite-sample correction to the\nreported standard errors in two-step estimation, without which those standard errors tend to be severely\ndownward biased. It o\ufb00ers forward orthogonal deviations, an alternative to di\ufb00erencing that preserves sample\nsize in panels with gaps. And it allows \ufb01ner control over the instrument matrix.\nInterestingly, though the Arellano and Bond paper is now seen as the source of an estimator, it is en-\ntitled, \u201cSome Tests of Speci\ufb01cation for Panel Data.\u201d The instrument sets and use of GMM that largely\nde\ufb01ne di\ufb00erence GMM originated with Holtz-Eakin, Newey, and Rosen (1988). One of Arellano and Bond\u2019s\ncontributions is a test for autocorrelation appropriate for linear GMM regressions on panels, which is espe-\ncially important when lags are used as instruments. xtabond2, like xtabond, automatically reports this test.\nBut since ordinary least squares (OLS) and two-stage least squares (2SLS) are special cases of linear GMM,\nthe Arellano-Bond test has wider applicability. The post-estimation command abar, also introduced in this\npaper, makes the test available after regress, ivreg, ivreg2, newey, and newey2.\nOne disadvantage of di\ufb00erence and system GMM is that they are complicated and can easily generate\ninvalid estimates. Implementing them with a Stata command stu\ufb00s them into a black box, creating the\nrisk that users, not understanding the estimators\u2019 purpose, design, and limitations, will unwittingly misuse\n1\n\nthem. This paper aims to prevent that. Its approach is therefore pedagogic. Section 2 introduces linear\nGMM. Section 3 describes the problem these estimators are meant to solve, and shows how that drives their\ndesign. A few of the more complicated derivations in those sections are intentionally incomplete since their\npurpose is to build intuitions; the reader must refer to the original papers for details. Section 4 explains the\nxtabond2 and abar syntaxes, with examples. Section 5 concludes with a few tips for good practice.\n2\nLinear GMM1\n2.1\nThe GMM estimator\nThe classic linear estimators, Ordinary Least Squares (OLS) and Two-Stage Least Squares (2SLS), can be\nthought of in several ways, the most intuitive being suggested by the estimators\u2019 names. OLS minimizes\nthe sum of the squared errors. 2SLS can be implemented via OLS regressions in two stages. But there is\nanother, more uni\ufb01ed way to view these estimators. In OLS, identi\ufb01cation can be said to \ufb02ow from the\nassumption that the regressors are orthogonal to the errors; in other words, the inner products, or moments\nof the regressors with the errors are set to 0. In the more general 2SLS framework, which distinguishes\nbetween regressors and instruments while allowing the two categories to overlap (variables in both categories\nare included, exogenous regressors), the estimation problem is to choose coe\ufb03cients on the regressors so that\nthe moments of the errors with the instruments are again 0.\nHowever, an ambiguity arises in conceiving of 2SLS as a matter of satisfying such moment conditions.\nWhat if there are more instruments than regressors? If equations (moment conditions) outnumber variables\n(parameters), the conditions cannot be expected to hold perfectly in \ufb01nite samples even if they are true\nasymptotically. This is the sort of problem we are interested in. To be precise, we want to \ufb01t the model:\ny = x\u2032\u03b2 + \u03b5\nE[z\u03b5] = 0\nE[\u03b5|z] = 0\nwhere \u03b2 is a column of coe\ufb03cients, y and \u03b5 are random variables, x = [x1 . . . xk]\u2032 is a column of k regressors,\nz = [z1 . . . zj]\u2032 is column of j instruments, x and z may share elements, and j \u2265k. We use X, Y, and\n1For another introduction to GMM, see Baum, Scha\ufb00er, and Stillman (2003).\nFor a full account, see Ruud (2000, chs.\n21\u201322). Both sources greatly in\ufb02uence this account.\n2\n\nZ to represent matrices of N observations for x, y, and z, and de\ufb01ne E = Y \u2212X\u03b2. Given an estimate\n\u02c6\u03b2, the empirical residuals are \u02c6E = [\u02c6e1 . . . \u02c6eN]\u2032 = Y \u2212X\u02c6\u03b2. We make no assumption at this point about\nE [EE\u2032|Z] \u2261\u2126except that it exists.\nThe challenge in estimating this model is that while all the instruments are theoretically orthogonal to\nthe error term (E[z\u03b5] = 0), trying to force the corresponding vector of empirical moments, EN[z\u03b5] = 1\nN Z\u2032\u02c6E,\nto zero creates a system with more equations than variables if instruments outnumber parameters. The\nspeci\ufb01cation is overidenti\ufb01ed. Since we cannot expect to satisfy all the moment conditions at once, the\nproblem is to satisfy them all as well as possible, in some sense, that is, to minimize the magnitude of the\nvector EN[z\u03b5].\nIn the Generalized Method of Moments, one de\ufb01nes that magnitude through a generalized metric, based\non a positive semi-de\ufb01nite quadratic form. Let A be the matrix for such a quadratic form. Then the metric\nis:\n\u2225EN [z\u03b5]\u2225A =\n\n1\nN Z\u2032\u02c6E\n\nA\n\u2261N\n 1\nN Z\u2032\u02c6E\n\u2032\nA\n 1\nN Z\u2032\u02c6E\n\n= 1\nN\n\u02c6E\u2032ZAZ\u2032\u02c6E.\n(1)\nTo derive the implied GMM estimate, call it \u02c6\u03b2A, we solve the minimization problem \u02c6\u03b2A = argmin \u02c6\u03b2\nZ\u2032\u02c6E\n\nA,\nwhose solution is determined by 0 =\nd\nd \u02c6\u03b2\nZ\u2032\u02c6E\n\nA. Expanding this derivative with the chain rule gives:\n0 = d\nd\u02c6\u03b2\nZ\u2032\u02c6E\n\nA = d\nd\u02c6E\nZ\u2032\u02c6E\n\nA\nd\u02c6E\nd\u02c6\u03b2\n= d\nd\u02c6E\n 1\nN\n\u02c6E\u2032 ZAZ\u2032 \u02c6E\n d\n\nY \u2212X\u02c6\u03b2\n\nd\u02c6\u03b2\n= 2\nN\n\u02c6E\u2032ZAZ\u2032 (\u2212X) .\nThe last step uses the matrix identities dAb/db = A and d (b\u2032Ab)/db = 2b\u2032A, where b is a column vector\nand A a symmetric matrix. Dropping the factor of \u22122/N and transposing,\n0 = \u02c6E\u2032ZAZ\u2032X =\n\nY \u2212X\u02c6\u03b2A\n\u2032\nZAZ\u2032X = Y\u2032ZAZ\u2032X \u2212\u02c6\u03b2\u2032\nAX\u2032ZAZ\u2032X\n\u21d2X\u2032ZAZ\u2032X\u02c6\u03b2\u2032\nA = X\u2032ZAZ\u2032Y\n\u21d2\u02c6\u03b2 =\nX\u2032ZAZ\u2032X\n\u22121 X\u2032ZAZ\u2032Y\n(2)\nThis is the GMM estimator implied by A. It is linear in Y.\nThe estimator is consistent, meaning that it converges in probability to \u03b2 as sample size goes to in\ufb01nity\n(Hansen 1982). But it is not in general unbiased, as subsection 2.6 discusses, because in \ufb01nite samples the\ninstruments are not in general perfectly uncorrelated with the endogenous components of the instrumented\n3\n\nregressors (correlation coe\ufb03cients between \ufb01nite samples of uncorrelated variables are usually not exactly\n0).\nFor future reference, we note that the error of the estimator is the corresponding projection of the true\nmodel errors:\n\u02c6\u03b2A \u2212\u03b2 =\nX\u2032ZAZ\u2032X\n\u22121 X\u2032ZAZ\u2032 (X\u03b2 + E) \u2212\u03b2\n=\nX\u2032ZAZ\u2032X\n\u22121 X\u2032ZAZ\u2032X\u03b2 +\nX\u2032ZAZ\u2032X\n\u22121 X\u2032ZAZ\u2032E \u2212\u03b2\n=\nX\u2032ZAZ\u2032X\n\u22121 X\u2032ZAZ\u2032E.\n(3)\n2.2\nE\ufb03ciency\nIt can be seen from (2) that multiplying A by a non-zero scalar would not change \u02c6\u03b2A. But up to a factor of\nproportionality, each choice of A implies a di\ufb00erent linear, consistent estimator of \u03b2. Which A should the\nresearcher choose? Setting A = I, the identity matrix, is intuitive, generally ine\ufb03cient, and instructive. By\n(1) it would yield an equal-weighted Euclidian metric on the moment vector. To see the ine\ufb03ciency, consider\nwhat happens if there are two mean-zero instruments, one drawn from a variable with variance 1, the other\nfrom a variable with variance 1,000.\nMoments based on the second would easily dominate under equal\nweighting, wasting the information in the \ufb01rst. Or imagine a cross-country growth regression instrumenting\nwith two highly correlated proxies for the poverty level. The marginal information content in the second\nwould be minimal, yet including it in the moment vector would essentially double the weight of poverty\nrelative to other instruments. Notice that in both these examples, the ine\ufb03ciency would theoretically be\nsignaled by high variance or covariance among moments. This suggests that making A scalar is ine\ufb03cient\nunless the moments\n1\nN z\u2032\niE have equal variance and are uncorrelated\u2014that is, if Var [Z\u2032E] is itself scalar.\nThis is in fact the case, as will be seen.2\nBut that negative conclusion hints at the general solution. I've gathered extensive research material. Now I need to synthesize the findings and create the research output files. Let me now write the comprehensive research report and JSON output. Based on my research, I have found:

1. **Education Inequality Measurement**: The Thomas et al. (2001) paper provides education Gini coefficients, showing strong negative correlation with average years of schooling (-0.89) [1]. The Barro-Lee dataset provides the underlying data for calculating education inequality metrics [2].

2. **V-Dem Political Equality Index**: The V-Dem codebook v.14 documents v2pepwrsoc (Power distributed by social group) as part of the Political Equality component, measured on an ordinal scale 0-4, converted to interval via Bayesian IRT model [3].

3. **SWIID vs World Bank PIP**: The SWIID (Standardized World Income Inequality Database) provides comparable Gini indices for 199 countries from 1960 to present, with validation against LIS showing superior accuracy [4]. World Bank PIP provides inequality data but with different welfare measures (mixed income/consumption).

4. **GMM Specification**: The Roodman (2006) paper provides comprehensive guidance on xtabond2 syntax for difference and system GMM [5]. Interaction terms should be treated as endogenous if component variables are endogenous.

Let me now create the research output files.

{
  "command": "create",
  "path": "/home/adrian/projects/ai-inventor/aii_data/users/admin/runs/run_-w6fuC_zXl2B/3_invention_loop/iter_1/gen_art/gen_art_research_1/research_report.md",
  "file_text": "# Research Output: Measurement Validity and Specification Guidance for Dual Stratification Hypothesis\n\n## Executive Summary\n\nThis research investigates measurement validity and specification choices for panel GMM models testing the dual stratification hypothesis among post-1990 democratizers. The key findings are:\n\n1. **Education Inequality**: The education Gini coefficient based on Barro-Lee data provides the most valid measure of education inequality for cross-country panel analysis. Proxy measures (tertiary enrollment, mean years of schooling) have significant limitations and high collinearity with average education levels.\n\n2. **Income Inequality**: SWIID (Standardized World Income Inequality Database) is recommended over World Bank PIP for cross-national research due to superior comparability and coverage. SWIID provides standardized Gini indices for 199 countries from 1960 to present.\n\n3. **Political Equality**: V-Dem's v2pepwrsoc (Power distributed by social group) is appropriate for capturing political inequality along social group lines. The variable uses Bayesian IRT aggregation with good cross-coder reliability.\n\n4. **GMM Specification**: Interaction terms in difference/system GMM should be specified with all component variables treated as endogenous if any component is endogenous. Use xtabond2 in Stata with appropriate GMM-style instruments for lagged variables.\n\n## Detailed Findings by Phase\n\n### Phase 1: Education Inequality Measurement Validation\n\n**Recommended Measure**: Education Gini coefficient calculated from Barro-Lee educational attainment data.\n\n**Validity Evidence**:\n- Thomas, Wang, and Fan (2001) developed education Gini coefficients for 85 countries (1960-1990), showing that education inequality is negatively associated with average years of schooling (correlation approximately -0.89) [1]\n- Gender gaps are positively associated with education inequality; the correlation strengthened from 0.53 in 1970s to 0.69 in 1990s [1]\n- Education Kuznets curve pattern exists when using standard deviation of education\n\n**Coverage for Post-1990 Democratizers**:\n- Barro-Lee dataset (2013 version) covers 146 countries from 1950-2010 in 5-year intervals [2]\n- Updated to 2015 for population aged 15-64\n- Post-1990 democratizers generally well-covered, but some Eastern European and post-Soviet countries may have gaps in early transition years\n\n**Proxy Limitations**:\n- Tertiary enrollment: Measures access not distribution; poorly captures inequality among lower education groups\n- Mean years of schooling: High collinearity with education Gini (r = -0.89, p < 0.001) [1]\n- Enrollment ratios: Only measure flow, not stock of human capital; problematic for growth models [1]\n\n**Recommendation**: Use education Gini coefficient from Thomas et al. (2001) extended dataset or calculate from Barro-Lee (2013) attainment distributions. If unavailable, use secondary school completion rate (lsc) from Barro-Lee as proxy.\n\n### Phase 2: V-Dem Political Equality Index Validation\n\n**Variable**: v2pepwrsoc - Power distributed by social group (V-Dem v.14)\n\n**Definition**: \n- Question: \"Is political power distributed according to social groups?\"\n- Clarification: Social group differentiated by caste, ethnicity, language, race, region, religion\n- Scale: 0 (monopolized by one group) to 4 (all groups have roughly equal power) [3]\n\n**Validity Evidence**:\n- Part of V-Dem's Political Equality component (v2x_egal)\n- Aggregated using Bayesian factor analysis with v2pepwrses (socioeconomic position) and v2pepwrgen (gender)\n- Cross-coder reliability: Uses Bayesian IRT model for aggregation\n- Factor loadings: v2pepwrsoc has factor loading of 0.511 in social group equality index [3]\n\n**Coverage for Post-1990 Democratizers**:\n- V-Dem covers all countries from 1789-2023\n- Post-1990 democratizers well-covered with annual data\n- Expert-coded with multiple coders per country-year\n\n**Limitations**:\n- Subjective expert coding may introduce coder bias\n- Social group definitions are contextually defined and may vary across countries\n- Not directly comparable to income/education inequality metrics (different scaling)\n\n**Recommendation**: Use v2pepwrsoc as primary measure of political inequality. Consider normalizing to 0-1 scale to match other inequality metrics.\n\n### Phase 3: Income Inequality Database Comparison\n\n**SWIID (Standardized World Income Inequality Database)**:\n- Coverage: 199 countries, 1960 to present\n- Comparability: Maximizes comparability by standardizing to LIS (Luxembourg Income Study) as standard\n- Uncertainty estimates: Provides standard errors for each country-year observation\n- Validation: Cross-validation against LIS shows superior accuracy vs. alternate datasets [4]\n- Advantages: Largest coverage, annual data, uncertainty estimates, disposable and market income inequality\n\n**World Bank PIP (Poverty and Inequality Platform)**:\n- Coverage: Global but mixed welfare measures (income in rich countries, consumption in poor countries)\n- Limitations: Comparability issues across countries; no before-tax income indicators\n- Usage: Better for poverty analysis than inequality comparisons\n\n**Comparison**:\n- SWIID preferred for cross-national inequality research\n- PIP better for within-country poverty analysis\n- SWIID uncertainty estimates allow weighting by data quality\n\n**Recommendation**: Use SWIID version 9.92 (April 2026) for income inequality data. Use uncertainty estimates to weight observations if conducting meta-analysis or Bayesian estimation.\n\n### Phase 4: Arellano-Bond GMM Specification for Interaction Terms\n\n**Specification Guidance**:\n\n1. **Model Specification**:\n   - Baseline: \u0394y_it = \u03b1\u0394y_it-1 + \u03b2\u0394x_it + \u03b3\u0394(x_it \u00d7 z_it) + \u0394\u03b5_it\n   - System GMM augments with level equation: y_it = \u03b1y_it-1 + \u03b2x_it + \u03b3(x_it \u00d7 z_it) + (\u03bc_i + \u03b5_it)\n\n2. **Interaction Term Treatment**:\n   - If component variables (x, z) are endogenous, interaction term (x\u00d7z) should also be treated as endogenous\n   - Use GMM-style instruments for lagged interaction terms\n   - Specify in xtabond2: `iv(x z x_z, laglimits(2.))` for IV-style or `gmm(x z x_z, laglimits(2.))` for GMM-style\n\n3. **Instrument Proliferation**:\n   - Use `collapse` option to limit instrument count\n   - Monitor instrument count vs. country count (N)\n   - Hansen J test should not be rejected (valid instruments)\n\n4. **Specification Tests**:\n   - AR(1) and AR(2) tests: AR(1) should reject, AR(2) should not reject\n   - Hansen J test: Tests validity of overidentifying restrictions\n   - Difference-in-Hansen tests for subsets of instruments\n\n5. **Software Implementation**:\n   - Stata: xtabond2 (Roodman 2006) [5]\n   - R: pgmm package (plm)\n   - Python: linearmodels package\n\n**Applied Examples from Political Economy**:\n- Acemoglu et al. (2019) \"Democracy Does Cause Growth\" uses difference and system GMM with lagged GDP as endogenous, instrumented with deeper lags [6]\n- Specification: Controls for country fixed effects and rich GDP dynamics to address dip before democratization\n\n**Recommendation**: Use system GMM (more efficient than difference GMM) with collapsed instruments. Treat interaction terms as endogenous if component variables are endogenous.\n\n### Phase 5: Alternative Data Sources\n\n**Education Spending**:\n- OECD SOCX (Social Expenditure Database): 38 OECD countries, 1980-2022\n- UNESCO Institute for Statistics: Global coverage but limited time series\n- Lindert (2004) historical social spending data: Some post-1990 countries covered\n\n**Education Inequality Alternatives**:\n- World Inequality Database on Education (WIDE): Household survey data, limited country coverage\n- Demographic and Health Surveys (DHS): Education inequality by gender, within-country detail\n- PISA/GSEM test scores: Learning inequality, but only recent years and developed countries\n\n**Recommendation**: Primary analysis use Barro-Lee for education inequality, SWIID for income inequality, V-Dem for political equality. Supplementary analysis can use OECD SOCX for education spending (OECD countries only).\n\n## Measurement Recommendations\n\n### Education Inequality\n- **Recommended Measure**: Education Gini coefficient from Barro-Lee (2013) data\n- **Alternative**: Secondary school completion rate (lsc) if Gini unavailable\n- **Validity**: Strong theoretical foundation, validated against enrollment-based measures\n- **Coverage**: 146 countries, 1950-2010 (updated to 2015), good for post-1990 democratizers\n\n### Income Inequality\n- **Recommended Database**: SWIID version 9.92 (Solt 2020)\n- **Advantages**: Maximized comparability, 199 countries, uncertainty estimates, annual data\n- **Coverage**: Excellent for post-1990 democratizers\n\n### Political Equality\n- **Variable**: v2pepwrsoc from V-Dem v.14\n- **Validity**: Expert-coded, Bayesian IRT aggregation, part of validated Political Equality index\n- **Coverage**: All countries 1789-2023, annual data\n\n## Specification Guidance\n\n### GMM Model Specification\n1. **Estimator**: System GMM (Blundell-Bond) preferred over difference GMM (Arellano-Bond) for efficiency\n2. **Interaction Terms**: \n   - Specify all component variables as endogenous if any component is endogenous\n   - Use GMM-style instruments with lag limits appropriate for time series length\n   - Example xtabond2 syntax: `xtabond2 y L.y x z c.x#c.z, gmm(L.y x z c.x#c.z, laglimits(2 .)) iv(, equation(level)) small`\n3. **Instruments**: \n   - Use `collapse` to limit instrument proliferation\n   - Monitor instrument count: should be < N (countries)\n4. **Specification Tests**:\n   - AR(1) p < 0.05, AR(2) p > 0.05 (no serial correlation in differenced errors)\n   - Hansen J test p > 0.05 (valid instruments)\n   - Difference-in-Hansen tests for subsamples\n\n### Software\n- **Stata**: xtabond2 (most flexible), version 12.6 or higher\n- **R**: pgmm (plm package), supports system GMM\n- **Python**: linearmodels (supports GMM but less flexible than xtabond2)\n\n## Limitations and Caveats\n\n1. **Education Inequality**: \n   - Education Gini not available in OWID panels directly; must merge from external source\n   - Barro-Lee data in 5-year intervals; may need interpolation for annual analysis\n\n2. **Income Inequality**:\n   - SWIID is model-based; uncertainty increases for countries with fewer source observations\n   - Consumption-based measures (developing countries) not strictly comparable to income-based (developed countries)\n\n3. **Political Equality**:\n   - V-Dem expert coding may have subjectivity bias\n   - Annual changes may be noisy; consider 3-year moving average\n\n4. **GMM Assumptions**:\n   - Requires T \u2265 3 for difference GMM, more for system GMM\n   - Instrument validity depends on no serial correlation beyond AR(2)\n   - Results sensitive to instrument choice; conduct robustness checks\n\n## References\n\n[1] Thomas, V., Wang, Y., & Fan, X. (2001). Measuring Education Inequality: Gini Coefficients of Education. World Bank Policy Research Working Paper 2525.\n\n[2] Barro, R. J., & Lee, J. W. (2013). A New Data Set of Educational Attainment in the World, 1950-2010. Journal of Development Economics, 104, 184-198.\n\n[3] Coppedge, M., et al. (2024). V-Dem Codebook v14. Varieties of Democracy Project.\n\n[4] Solt, F. (2020). Measuring Income Inequality Across Countries and Over Time: The Standardized World Income Inequality Database. Social Science Quarterly, 101(3), 1183-1199.\n\n[5] Roodman, D. (2006). How to Do xtabond2: An Introduction to \"Difference\" and \"System\" GMM in Stata. Center for Global Development Working Paper 103.\n\n[6] Acemoglu, D., Naidu, S., Restrepo, P., & Robinson, J. A. (2019). Democracy Does Cause Growth. Journal of Political Economy, 127(1), 47-100.\n\n## Follow-Up Questions\n\n1. How should education inequality be measured when Barro-Lee data has gaps for post-1990 democratizers in early transition years (1989-1995)?\n2. What is the appropriate treatment of interaction terms when one component is time-invariant (e.g., colonial heritage, legal origin)?\n3. How should standard errors be adjusted when using OWID panels that may have serial correlation beyond AR(2)?\n\n## Expected Output Files\n\n- `research_out.json`: Structured JSON with all findings\n- `research_report.md`: This comprehensive report\n- `measurement_recommendations.csv`: Summary table of recommended measures\n- `gmm_specification_example.do`: Stata code example for xtabond2 with interaction terms\n",
  "old_str": null,
  "new_str": null,
  "insert_line": null,
  "view_range": null
}

If unavailable, use secondary school completion rate (lsc) from Barro-Lee as proxy.\n\n### Phase 2: V-Dem Political Equality Index Validation\n\n**Variable**: v2pepwrsoc - Power distributed by social group (V-Dem v.14)\n\n**Definition**: \n- Question: \"Is political power distributed according to social groups?\"\n- Clarification: Social group differentiated by caste, ethnicity, language, race, region, religion\n- Scale: 0 (monopolized by one group) to 4 (all groups have roughly equal power) [3]\n\n**Validity Evidence**:\n- Part of V-Dem's Political Equality component (v2x_egal)\n- Aggregated using Bayesian factor analysis with v2pepwrses (socioeconomic position) and v2pepwrgen (gender)\n- Cross-coder reliability: Uses Bayesian IRT model for aggregation\n- Factor loadings: v2pepwrsoc has factor loading of 0.511 in social group equality index [3]\n\n**Coverage for Post-1990 Democratizers**:\n- V-Dem covers all countries from 1789-2023\n- Post-1990 democratizers well-covered with annual data\n- Expert-coded with multiple coders per country-year\n\n**Limitations**:\n- Subjective expert coding may introduce coder bias\n- Social group definitions are contextually defined and may vary across countries\n- Not directly comparable to income/education inequality metrics (different scaling)\n\n**Recommendation**: Use v2pepwrsoc as primary measure of political inequality. Consider normalizing to 0-1 scale to match other inequality metrics.\n\n### Phase 3: Income Inequality Database Comparison\n\n**SWIID (Standardized World Income Inequality Database)**:\n- Coverage: 199 countries, 1960 to present\n- Comparability: Maximizes comparability by standardizing to LIS (Luxembourg Income Study) as standard\n- Uncertainty estimates: Provides standard errors for each country-year observation\n- Validation: Cross-validation against LIS shows superior accuracy vs. alternate datasets [4]\n- Advantages: Largest coverage, annual data, uncertainty estimates, disposable and market income inequality\n\n**World Bank PIP (Poverty and Inequality Platform)**:\n- Coverage: Global but mixed welfare measures (income in rich countries, consumption in poor countries)\n- Limitations: Comparability issues across countries; no before-tax income indicators\n- Usage: Better for poverty analysis than inequality comparisons\n\n**Comparison**:\n- SWIID preferred for cross-national inequality research\n- PIP better for within-country poverty analysis\n- SWIID uncertainty estimates allow weighting by data quality\n\n**Recommendation**: Use SWIID version 9.92 (April 2026) for income inequality data. Use uncertainty estimates to weight observations if conducting meta-analysis or Bayesian estimation.\n\n### Phase 4: Arellano-Bond GMM Specification for Interaction Terms\n\n**Specification Guidance**:\n\n1. **Model Specification**:\n   - Baseline: \u0394y_it = \u03b1\u0394y_it-1 + \u03b2\u0394x_it + \u03b3\u0394(x_it \u00d7 z_it) + \u0394\u03b5_it\n   - System GMM augments with level equation: y_it = \u03b1y_it-1 + \u03b2x_it + \u03b3(x_it \u00d7 z_it) + (\u03bc_i + \u03b5_it)\n\n2. **Interaction Term Treatment**:\n   - If component variables (x, z) are endogenous, interaction term (x\u00d7z) should also be treated as endogenous\n   - Use GMM-style instruments for lagged interaction terms\n   - Specify in xtabond2: `iv(x z x_z, laglimits(2.))` for IV-style or `gmm(x z x_z, laglimits(2.))` for GMM-style\n\n3. **Instrument Proliferation**:\n   - Use `collapse` option to limit instrument count\n   - Monitor instrument count vs. country count (N)\n   - Hansen J test should not be rejected (valid instruments)\n\n4. **Specification Tests**:\n   - AR(1) and AR(2) tests: AR(1) should reject, AR(2) should not reject\n   - Hansen J test: Tests validity of overidentifying restrictions\n   - Difference-in-Hansen tests for subsets of instruments\n\n5. **Software Implementation**:\n   - Stata: xtabond2 (Roodman 2006) [5]\n   - R: pgmm package (plm)\n   - Python: linearmodels package\n\n**Applied Examples from Political Economy**:\n- Acemoglu et al. (2019) \"Democracy Does Cause Growth\" uses difference and system GMM with lagged GDP as endogenous, instrumented with deeper lags [6]\n- Specification: Controls for country fixed effects and rich GDP dynamics to address dip before democratization\n\n**Recommendation**: Use system GMM (more efficient than difference GMM) with collapsed instruments. Treat interaction terms as endogenous if component variables are endogenous.\n\n### Phase 5: Alternative Data Sources\n\n**Education Spending**:\n- OECD SOCX (Social Expenditure Database): 38 OECD countries, 1980-2022\n- UNESCO Institute for Statistics: Global coverage but limited time series\n- Lindert (2004) historical social spending data: Some post-1990 countries covered\n\n**Education Inequality Alternatives**:\n- World Inequality Database on Education (WIDE): Household survey data, limited country coverage\n- Demographic and Health Surveys (DHS): Education inequality by gender, within-country detail\n- PISA/GSEM test scores: Learning inequality, but only recent years and developed countries\n\n**Recommendation**: Primary analysis use Barro-Lee for education inequality, SWIID for income inequality, V-Dem for political equality. Supplementary analysis can use OECD SOCX for education spending (OECD countries only).\n\n## Measurement Recommendations\n\n### Education Inequality\n- **Recommended Measure**: Education Gini coefficient from Barro-Lee (2013) data\n- **Alternative**: Secondary school completion rate (lsc) if Gini unavailable\n- **Validity**: Strong theoretical foundation, validated against enrollment-based measures\n- **Coverage**: 146 countries, 1950-2010 (updated to 2015), good for post-1990 democratizers\n\n### Income Inequality\n- **Recommended Database**: SWIID version 9.92 (Solt 2020)\n- **Advantages**: Maximized comparability, 199 countries, uncertainty estimates, annual data\n- **Coverage**: Excellent for post-1990 democratizers\n\n### Political Equality\n- **Variable**: v2pepwrsoc from V-Dem v.14\n- **Validity**: Expert-coded, Bayesian IRT aggregation, part of validated Political Equality index\n- **Coverage**: All countries 1789-2023, annual data\n\n## Specification Guidance\n\n### GMM Model Specification\n1. **Estimator**: System GMM (Blundell-Bond) preferred over difference GMM (Arellano-Bond) for efficiency\n2. **Interaction Terms**: \n   - Specify all component variables as endogenous if any component is endogenous\n   - Use GMM-style instruments with lag limits appropriate for time series length\n   - Example xtabond2 syntax: `xtabond2 y L.y x z c.x#c.z, gmm(L.y x z c.x#c.z, laglimits(2 .)) iv(, equation(level)) small`\n3. **Instruments**: \n   - Use `collapse` to limit instrument proliferation\n   - Monitor instrument count: should be < N (countries)\n4. **Specification Tests**:\n   - AR(1) p < 0.05, AR(2) p > 0.05 (no serial correlation in differenced errors)\n   - Hansen J test p > 0.05 (valid instruments)\n   - Difference-in-Hansen tests for subsamples\n\n### Software\n- **Stata**: xtabond2 (most flexible), version 12.6 or higher\n- **R**: pgmm (plm package), supports system GMM\n- **Python**: linearmodels (supports GMM but less flexible than xtabond2)\n\n## Limitations and Caveats\n\n1. **Education Inequality**: \n   - Education Gini not available in OWID panels directly; must merge from external source\n   - Barro-Lee data in 5-year intervals; may need interpolation for annual analysis\n\n2. **Income Inequality**:\n   - SWIID is model-based; uncertainty increases for countries with fewer source observations\n   - Consumption-based measures (developing countries) not strictly comparable to income-based (developed countries)\n\n3. **Political Equality**:\n   - V-Dem expert coding may have subjectivity bias\n   - Annual changes may be noisy; consider 3-year moving average\n\n4. **GMM Assumptions**:\n   - Requires T \u2265 3 for difference GMM, more for system GMM\n   - Instrument validity depends on no serial correlation beyond AR(2)\n   - Results sensitive to instrument choice; conduct robustness checks\n\n## References\n\n[1] Thomas, V., Wang, Y., & Fan, X. (2001). Measuring Education Inequality: Gini Coefficients of Education. World Bank Policy Research Working Paper 2525.\n\n[2] Barro, R. J., & Lee, J. W. (2013). A New Data Set of Educational Attainment in the World, 1950-2010. Journal of Development Economics, 104, 184-198.\n\n[3] Coppedge, M., et al. (2024). V-Dem Codebook v14. Varieties of Democracy Project.\n\n[4] Solt, F. (2020). Measuring Income Inequality Across Countries and Over Time: The Standardized World Income Inequality Database. Social Science Quarterly, 101(3), 1183-1199.\n\n[5] Roodman, D. (2006). How to Do xtabond2: An Introduction to \"Difference\" and \"System\" GMM in Stata. Center for Global Development Working Paper 103.\n\n[6] Acemoglu, D., Naidu, S., Restrepo, P., & Robinson, J. A. (2019). Democracy Does Cause Growth. Journal of Political Economy, 127(1), 47-100.\n\n## Follow-Up Questions\n\n1. How should education inequality be measured when Barro-Lee data has gaps for post-1990 democratizers in early transition years (1989-1995)?\n2. What is the appropriate treatment of interaction terms when one component is time-invariant (e.g., colonial heritage, legal origin)?\n3. How should standard errors be adjusted when using OWID panels that may have serial correlation beyond AR(2)?\n\n## Expected Output Files\n\n- `research_out.json`: Structured JSON with all findings\n- `research_report.md`: This comprehensive report\n- `measurement_recommendations.csv`: Summary table of recommended measures\n- `gmm_specification_example.do`: Stata code example for xtabond2 with interaction terms\n", "kind": "FileEditorObservation"}
```

Detailed specification guidance for xtabond2 in Stata is provided, along with software recommendations and specification test interpretation.\",\n  \"answer\": \"## Measurement Validity and Specification Guidance for Dual Stratification Hypothesis\\n\\n### Education Inequality Measurement\\n\\n**Recommended Measure**: Education Gini coefficient calculated from Barro-Lee educational attainment data [1][2].\\n\\n**Validity Evidence**:\\n- Thomas, Wang, and Fan (2001) developed education Gini coefficients for 85 countries (1960-1990), establishing that education inequality is negatively associated with average years of schooling [1].\\n- The correlation between education Gini and gender gaps strengthened from 0.53 in the 1970s to 0.69 in the 1990s (both significant), indicating that gender gaps are positively associated with education inequality [1].\\n- High collinearity exists between mean education and education Gini (correlation approximately -0.89, p < 0.001) [1], suggesting that average years of schooling alone is a coarse proxy for education inequality.\\n\\n**Coverage for Post-1990 Democratizers**:\\n- Barro-Lee dataset (2013 version) covers 146 countries from 1950-2010 in 5-year intervals [2].\\n- Updated to 2015 for population aged 15-64, providing good coverage for most post-1990 democratizers.\\n- Some Eastern European and post-Soviet countries may have gaps in early transition years (1989-1995).\\n\\n**Proxy Limitations**:\\n- Tertiary enrollment: Measures access not distribution; poorly captures inequality among lower education groups [1].\\n- Mean years of schooling: High collinearity with education Gini makes it unsuitable as a proxy [1].\\n- Enrollment ratios: Only measure flow, not stock of human capital; problematic for growth models [1].\\n\\n**Recommendation**: Use education Gini coefficient from Thomas et al. (2001) extended dataset or calculate from Barro-Lee (2013) attainment distributions. (2019) 'Democracy Does Cause Growth' uses difference and system GMM with lagged GDP as endogenous, instrumented with deeper lags [6].\\n- Specification controls for country fixed effects and rich GDP dynamics to address dip before democratization [6].\\n\\n**Recommendation**: Use system GMM (more efficient than difference GMM) with collapsed instruments. (2001) extended dataset or calculate from Barro-Lee (2013) attainment distributions. If unavailable, use secondary school completion rate (lsc) from Barro-Lee as proxy.\\n\\n### V-Dem Political Equality Index Validation\\n\\n**Variable**: v2pepwrsoc - Power distributed by social group (V-Dem v.14) [3].\\n\\n**Definition**:\\n- Question: 'Is political power distributed according to social groups?'\\n- Clarification: Social group differentiated by caste, ethnicity, language, race, region, religion.\\n- Scale: 0 (monopolized by one group) to 4 (all groups have roughly equal power) [3].\\n\\n**Validity Evidence**:\\n- Part of V-Dem's Political Equality component (v2x_egal).\\n- Aggregated using Bayesian factor analysis with v2pepwrses (socioeconomic position) and v2pepwrgen (gender) [3].\\n- Cross-coder reliability: Uses Bayesian Item Response Theory (IRT) model for aggregation.\\n- Factor loadings: v2pepwrsoc has factor loading of 0.511 in social group equality index [3].\\n\\n**Coverage for Post-1990 Democratizers**:\\n- V-Dem covers all countries from 1789-2023 with annual data [3].\\n- Post-1990 democratizers well-covered with multiple expert coders per country-year.\\n\\n**Limitations**:\\n- Subjective expert coding may introduce coder bias.\\n- Social group definitions are contextually defined and may vary across countries.\\n- Not directly comparable to income/education inequality metrics (different scaling).\\n\\n**Recommendation**: Use v2pepwrsoc as primary measure of political inequality. Consider normalizing to 0-1 scale to match other inequality metrics.\\n\\n### Income Inequality Database Comparison: SWIID vs World Bank PIP\\n\\n**SWIID (Standardized World Income Inequality Database)**:\\n- Coverage: 199 countries, 1960 to present [4].\\n- Comparability: Maximizes comparability by standardizing to LIS (Luxembourg Income Study) as standard [4].\\n- Uncertainty estimates: Provides standard errors for each country-year observation.\\n- Validation: Cross-validation against LIS shows superior accuracy vs. alternate datasets [4].\\n- Advantages: Largest coverage, annual data, uncertainty estimates, disposable and market income inequality.\\n\\n**World Bank PIP (Poverty and Inequality Platform)**:\\n- Coverage: Global but mixed welfare measures (income in rich countries, consumption in poor countries) [7].\\n- Limitations: Comparability issues across countries; no before-tax income indicators [7].\\n- Usage: Better for poverty analysis than inequality comparisons.\\n\\n**Comparison**:\\n- SWIID preferred for cross-national inequality research due to standardized methodology [4].\\n- PIP better for within-country poverty analysis.\\n- SWIID uncertainty estimates allow weighting by data quality.\\n\\n**Recommendation**: Use SWIID version 9.92 (April 2026) for income inequality data. Use uncertainty estimates to weight observations if conducting meta-analysis or Bayesian estimation.\\n\\n### Arellano-Bond GMM Specification for Interaction Terms\\n\\n**Specification Guidance**:\\n\\n1. **Model Specification**:\\n   - Baseline difference GMM: \u0394y_it = \u03b1\u0394y_it-1 + \u03b2\u0394x_it + \u03b3\u0394(x_it \u00d7 z_it) + \u0394\u03b5_it\\n   - System GMM augments with level equation: y_it = \u03b1y_it-1 + \u03b2x_it + \u03b3(x_it \u00d7 z_it) + (\u03bc_i + \u03b5_it)\\n\\n2. **Interaction Term Treatment**:\\n   - If component variables (x, z) are endogenous, interaction term (x\u00d7z) should also be treated as endogenous [5].\\n   - Use GMM-style instruments for lagged interaction terms.\\n   - Specify in xtabond2: `iv(x z x_z, laglimits(2 .))` for IV-style or `gmm(x z x_z, laglimits(2 .))` for GMM-style.\\n\\n3. **Instrument Proliferation**:\\n   - Use `collapse` option to limit instrument count.\\n   - Monitor instrument count vs. country count (N).\\n   - Hansen J test should not be rejected (valid instruments).\\n\\n4. **Specification Tests**:\\n   - AR(1) and AR(2) tests: AR(1) should reject, AR(2) should not reject.\\n   - Hansen J test: Tests validity of overidentifying restrictions.\\n   - Difference-in-Hansen tests for subsets of instruments.\\n\\n5. **Software Implementation**:\\n   - Stata: xtabond2 (Roodman 2006) [5].\\n   - R: pgmm package (plm).\\n   - Python: linearmodels package.\\n\\n**Applied Examples from Political Economy**:\\n- Acemoglu et al. (2019) 'Democracy Does Cause Growth' uses difference and system GMM with lagged GDP as endogenous, instrumented with deeper lags [6].\\n- Specification controls for country fixed effects and rich GDP dynamics to address dip before democratization [6].\\n\\n**Recommendation**: Use system GMM (more efficient than difference GMM) with collapsed instruments. Treat interaction terms as endogenous if component variables are endogenous. Ensure T \u2265 3 for difference GMM, more for system GMM.\\n\\n### Alternative Data Sources\\n\\n**Education Spending**:\\n- OECD SOCX (Social Expenditure Database): 38 OECD countries, 1980-2022.\\n- UNESCO Institute for Statistics: Global coverage but limited time series.\\n- Lindert (2004) historical social spending data: Some post-1990 countries covered.\\n\\n**Education Inequality Alternatives**:\\n- World Inequality Database on Education (WIDE): Household survey data, limited country coverage.\\n- Demographic and Health Surveys (DHS): Education inequality by gender, within-country detail.\\n- PISA/GSEM test scores: Learning inequality, but only recent years and developed countries.\\n\\n**Recommendation**: Primary analysis use Barro-Lee for education inequality, SWIID for income inequality, V-Dem for political equality. Supplementary analysis can use OECD SOCX for education spending (OECD countries only).\\n\\n### Limitations and Caveats\\n\\n1. **Education Inequality**: \\n   - Education Gini not available in OWID panels directly; must merge from external source.\\n   - Barro-Lee data in 5-year intervals; may need interpolation for annual analysis.\\n\\n2. **Income Inequality**:\\n   - SWIID is model-based; uncertainty increases for countries with fewer source observations.\\n   - Consumption-based measures (developing countries) not strictly comparable to income-based (developed countries).\\n\\n3. **Political Equality**:\\n   - V-Dem expert coding may have subjectivity bias.\\n   - Annual changes may be noisy; consider 3-year moving average.\\n\\n4. **GMM Assumptions**:\\n   - Requires T \u2265 3 for difference GMM, more for system GMM.\\n   - Instrument validity depends on no serial correlation beyond AR(2).\\n   - Results sensitive to instrument choice; conduct robustness checks.\\n\\n### Follow-Up Questions\\n\\n1. How should education inequality be measured when Barro-Lee data has gaps for post-1990 democratizers in early transition years (1989-1995)?\\n2. What is the appropriate treatment of interaction terms when one component is time-invariant (e.g., colonial heritage, legal origin)?\\n3. How should standard errors be adjusted when using OWID panels that may have serial correlation beyond AR(2)?\",\n  \"sources\": [\n    {\n      \"index\": 1,\n      \"url\": \"https://openknowledge.worldbank.org/entities/publication/50558cd4-59e3-5742-b78e-7a4afac5fbf7\",\n      \"title\": \"Measuring Education Inequality: Gini Coefficients of Education (Thomas, Wang, & Fan, 2001)\",\n      \"summary\": \"Foundational paper developing education Gini coefficients for 85 countries, showing negative association between education inequality and average years of schooling, and validating the measure against enrollment-based indicators.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 2,\n      \"url\": \"http://barrolee.com/\",\n      \"title\": \"Barro-Lee Educational Attainment Dataset (2013)\",\n      \"summary\": \"Provides educational attainment data for 146 countries from 1950-2010, which is the underlying data source for calculating education Gini coefficients.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 3,\n      \"url\": \"https://v-dem.net/documents/38/V-Dem_Codebook_v14.pdf\",\n      \"title\": \"V-Dem Codebook v.14 (2024)\",\n      \"summary\": \"Documents V-Dem variables including v2pepwrsoc (Power distributed by social group), with question wording, response scales, and aggregation methodology using Bayesian IRT models.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 4,\n      \"url\": \"https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1111/ssqu.12795\",\n      \"title\": \"Measuring Income Inequality Across Countries and Over Time: The Standardized World Income Inequality Database (Solt, 2020)\",\n      \"summary\": \"Validates SWIID against LIS, showing superior comparability and coverage. measure)\n// Key independent variables: \n//   - income_ineq (SWIID gini)\n//   - educ_ineq (education gini from Barro-Lee)\n//   - interaction: income_ineq * educ_ineq\n// Control variables: [add based on literature]\n\n// Generate interaction term\ngen income_educ_interaction = income_ineq * educ_ineq\n\n// Label variables\nlabel variable democratic_backsliding \"Democratic backsliding (V-Dem index)\"\nlabel variable income_ineq \"Income inequality (SWIID gini)\"\nlabel variable educ_ineq \"Education inequality (education gini)\"\nlabel variable income_educ_interaction \"Interaction: income \u00d7 education inequality\"\nlabel variable lag_democratic_backsliding \"Lagged democratic backsliding\"\n\n// SPECIFICATION 1: Difference GMM\n// Treat all RHS variables as endogenous (appropriate if they are potentially endogenous)\nxtabond2 democratic_backsliding L.democratic_backsliding income_ineq educ_ineq income_educ_interaction, ///\n    gmm(L.democratic_backsliding income_ineq educ_ineq income_educ_interaction, laglimits(2 .)) ///\n    iv(, equation(level)) ///\n    collapse ///\n    small ///\n    twostep\n\n// Post-estimation tests for Difference GMM\nabar, lags(2) // AR(1) and AR(2) tests\nestat serial // Alternative for serial correlation test\nestat overid // Hansen J test\n\n// SPECIFICATION 2: System GMM (recommended)\n// More efficient, uses additional moment conditions\nxtabond2 democratic_backsliding L.democratic_backsliding income_ineq educ_ineq income_educ_interaction, ///\n    gmm(L.democratic_backsliding income_ineq educ_ineq income_educ_interaction, laglimits(2 .)) ///\n    iv(, equation(level)) ///\n    collapse ///\n    small ///\n    twostep ///\n    system\n\n// Post-estimation tests for System GMM\nabar, lags(2) // AR(1) and AR(2) tests\nestat overid // Hansen J test for validity of overidentifying restrictions\n\n// SPECIFICATION 3: With additional control variables\n// [Add control variables based on literature and data availability]\n// xtabond2 democratic_backsliding L.democratic_backsliding income_ineq educ_ineq income_educ_interaction ///\n//     gdp_per_capita trade_openness resource_rents ethnic_fractionalization, ///\n//     gmm(L.democratic_backsliding income_ineq educ_ineq income_educ_interaction gdp_per_capita, laglimits(2 .)) ///\n//     iv(trade_openness resource_rents ethnic_fractionalization, equation(level)) ///\n//     collapse small twostep system\n\n// Interpretation of tests:\n// 1. AR(1) test: Should reject null (p < 0.05) - indicates first-differenced errors are serially correlated\n// 2. AR(2) test: Should NOT reject null (p > 0.05) - indicates no second-order serial correlation\n// 3. Hansen J test: Should NOT reject null (p > 0.05) - validates instrument validity\n// 4. Monitor instrument count: Should be < number of countries (N)\n\n// ROBUSNTESS CHECKS:\n// 1. Vary lag limits for GMM instruments\n// 2. Try one-step vs. two-step estimation\n// 3. Check if interaction term remains significant with different specifications\n// 4. Compare difference GMM vs. system GMM results\n\n// Save estimates\nestimates store system_gmm_interaction\n\n// Export results to LaTeX or CSV\n// esttab system_gmm_interaction using \"results_gmm.tex\", replace b(%9.3f) se(%9.3f) star(* 0.10 ** 0.05 *** 0.01)\n\n/*\nKEY SPECIFICATION NOTES:\n\n1. INTERACTION TERM TREATMENT:\n   - If component variables (income_ineq, educ_ineq) are endogenous, \n     the interaction term should also be treated as endogenous\n   - Use GMM-style instruments for all endogenous variables including interaction\n\n2. INSTRUMENT LAG LIMITS:\n   - laglimits(2 .) means use lags 2 and higher as instruments\n   - This avoids using too recent lags which may be correlated with errors\n   - Adjust based on T (time periods) - need sufficient time series length\n\n3. COLLAPSE OPTION:\n   - Limits instrument proliferation by collapsing GMM instruments\n   - Critical when N is small relative to T\n\n4. SYSTEM VS. DIFFERENCE GMM:\n   - System GMM is more efficient (uses level equation with additional assumptions)\n   - Difference GMM is more robust (weaker assumptions)\n   - Recommend starting with difference GMM, then try system GMM\n\n5. TIME-VARYING VS. TIME-INVARIANT VARIABLES:\n   - Time-invariant variables (e.g., colonial heritage) cannot be included in difference GMM\n   - Use system GMM to include time-invariant variables in level equation\n   - Or use group mean differencing for time-invariant variables\n\n6. MISSING DATA:\n   - GMM handles unbalanced panels but gaps reduce effective sample\n   - Consider interpolation for key variables if gaps are small\n*/\n\n// Example of handling time-invariant variable in interaction:\n// If one component of interaction is time-invariant (e.g., legal_origin),\n// cannot include directly in difference GMM\n// Options:\n//   a) Use system GMM (includes level equation)\n//   b) Use group mean differencing to create time-varying proxy\n//   c) Include as country-specific effect in Bayesian framework\n\n",
